The scene uses procedural primitives (cube faces map the full texture, so only the camera-facing faces are tiled correctly) and is not a high-end capture: as with the other goldens, a hardware-GPU reference is still to be recorded.

## ADR-0139 — Material occlusion from the ORM red channel

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: VICTUS materials had no ambient-occlusion input, so the cavity darkening that scanned (ambientCG, Poly Haven) and Substance-authored materials ship as an AO map was lost, and imported glTF `occlusionTexture`s were ignored. SSAO only covers screen-space geometry and is off on Low. glTF 2.0 defines the occlusion texture as the red channel, affecting indirect light only ("Direct lighting is not affected"), with an effective value of `1.0 + strength * (occlusionTexture - 1.0)`, and notes that it can share one texture with the metallic-roughness channels (G, B) ([glTF 2.0 specification](https://registry.khronos.org/glTF/specs/2.0/glTF-2.0.html)). Unreal's material convention is the same "ORM" packing (R occlusion, G roughness, B metallic) into a linear mask texture ([Epic Developer Community, Cinematic Master Materials](https://dev.epicgames.com/community/learning/tutorials/7d0y/unreal-engine-cinematic-master-materials-a-step-by-step-ue-guide-i-icvr)); Unity HDRP packs AO into the green channel of its Mask Map ([Texture Channel Packer presets](https://thetechnicalartist.com/tools/channel-packer/index.html)).
- Decision: `.vmat` gains `occlusion_strength` (0..1, default 0, written only when non-zero or overridden by an instance; `MaterialField::OcclusionStrength`), and pbr.frag computes `occlusion = 1 + strength * (mr.r - 1)` from the red channel of the metallic-roughness texture it already samples, so there is no new binding or fetch. The value multiplies the existing occlusion term (ambient, environment specular, probe and lightmap ambient), not direct lights. The uniform carries it in the spare `blend.y`. The glTF importer maps `occlusionTexture`: if it is the same image as the metallic-roughness texture, the texture is kept and `strength` is copied; a separate image of the same size is merged into the red channel of the metallic-roughness image (one cooked texture, cached per pair); an occlusion image without a metallic-roughness texture becomes one with G = B = 255 (roughness and metallic factors unchanged); a size mismatch warns and skips occlusion. The cooked model format is version 8 (occlusion strength after alpha cutoff). Studio's Material Editor has an Occlusion slider.
- Alternatives: a fifth texture slot (one more sampler binding in every PBR, surface-shader and FP16 pipeline layout, and one more fetch per pixel); applying AO to direct light too, as some engines allow for stylised looks (not physically motivated and contrary to glTF); resampling mismatched occlusion images to the metallic-roughness size (a CPU resize at import; rare in practice, so it waits for a real asset that needs it).
- Consequences: imported glTF models and scanned materials keep their baked cavity occlusion at no runtime cost; in the GPU test an ORM red of 64 cuts ambient-only output from 166 to 72 (strength 1) and 133 (0.5), while a sunlit plane keeps 216 of 226. Materials without the field render exactly as before (default 0). Occlusion uses the metallic-roughness uv (no `texCoord` 1 support), and existing cooked models re-cook once because of the format version.

## ADR-0138 — Baked probes on every tier, L1 on Low

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: the Low preset had `irradiance_volumes` off, which also switched off the probes baked with the lightmap (ADR-0133) and their sun occlusion (ADR-0136): on phones, dynamic objects in a baked scene took flat sky ambient and the full sun under a roof. The cost that justified turning it off belongs to hand-placed `IrradianceVolume` components (a runtime cubemap capture per probe, then 18 fetches per lit pixel); the lightmap's probes cost no capture, and lightmapped pixels never sample them, so only dynamic-object pixels pay. Unity's URP lets a project choose the spherical-harmonic bands of its probe data, "Spherical Harmonics L1" or "L2", where "L2 provides more precise results, but uses more system resources" ([URP asset reference](https://docs.unity3d.com/6000.2/Documentation//Manual/urp/universalrp-asset.html)).
- Decision: `RenderSettings::baked_probes` (on in every preset) governs volumes passed with `set_irradiance_volume(..., baked = true)`, which the runtime does for the lightmap's probes; `irradiance_volumes` keeps governing hand-placed volumes. `RenderSettings::probe_sh_bands` (2 = L2, 1 = L1; Low uses 1) sets `gi_min.w = 2`, and pbr.frag then fetches only the four band 0-1 rows (8 fetches instead of 18; row 1 still carries the probe sun visibility). The L2 path's maths is unchanged. Tiers with `irradiance_volumes` off no longer spend the capture budget on hand-placed volumes they never draw. The render path report shows `irradiance_volume` as reduced ("N baked probes, L1 only") on Low.
- Alternatives: per-object SH evaluated on the CPU at the bounds centre, as Unity's legacy probes do (no texture fetch, but a large object gets one value and needs per-instance SH data, 27 floats with L2); per-vertex SH (needs vertex-stage texture bindings in every vertex shader); keeping Low on sky ambient (the probe occlusion would stay unavailable on phones, where the realtime shadow distance is shortest).
- Consequences: Low dynamic objects get baked bounce light and probe sun occlusion for 8 fetches per pixel; in the GPU test the cube's red bounce is 152 with L1 against 144 with L2. L1 loses the directional detail of the L2 band (sharp sky/ground contrast is softer). Hand-placed volumes stay off on Low.

## ADR-0137 — FP16 shading behind a capability check

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: every VICTUS shader ran in FP32 (audit item A4). On mobile GPUs half precision doubles ALU throughput and cuts register pressure, so it is one of the larger shading wins for the Low and Medium tiers. Godot added explicit FP16 types to its mobile renderer, reporting about 10 % on an Adreno 640 and up to about 25 % on a Mali-G710, with 30 to 50 % less register pressure; it chose explicit `float16_t` over `RelaxedPrecision` because SPIRV-Cross drops the latter, so Metal would never see it ([Godot PR #107119](https://github.com/godotengine/godot/pull/107119)). Unreal 5.8 also ships explicit FP16 shading ([Unreal Engine 5.8 release notes](https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/unreal-engine-5-8-release-notes)). Filament's mobile path clamps the perceptual roughness to 0.089, so that roughness^4 stays above the smallest normal half-float, and computes 1 - (n.h)^2 as |n x h|^2 in FP32, against 0.045 on desktop ([Filament, roughness remapping and clamping](https://google.github.io/filament/Filament.html)).
- Decision: pbr.frag carries the `VICTUS_FP16_VARIANT` marker, and `tools/shaders/compile_shaders.py` also builds every marked shader as `<name>#fp16` with `-DVICTUS_FP16=1` (its digest covers the defines). Under `VICTUS_FP16`, `GL_EXT_shader_explicit_arithmetic_types_float16` maps `mfloat`/`mvecN` to `float16_t`/`f16vecN`; `brdf()` computes F, D (Filament's GGX form, clamped at 60000), G and the diffuse weight in half precision with the 0.089 roughness clamp, then multiplies by the FP32 radiance so that bright lights cannot overflow; `env_brdf` uses the same types. Positions, shadow coordinates, fog, exposure and the light loop stay FP32. The default build is unchanged. The RHI asks SDL_GPU for Vulkan 1.2 `shaderFloat16` through `SDL_GPUVulkanOptions` (the feature struct is declared layout-compatible, so the Vulkan headers are not needed), falls back to a device without it, and sets `DeviceCapabilities::shader_float16` only for Vulkan devices that were created with it (`DeviceDesc::shader_float16`, `VICTUS_NO_FP16=1` turns it off). `SceneRendererOptions::half_precision` selects `pbr.frag#fp16` when the device has the feature; `player::half_precision_by_default` turns it on for phones and the Low and Medium tiers (the same rule as compact HDR); `victus run --fp16 on|off|auto` overrides it; the render path report has a `shading_precision` feature. Surface shaders stay FP32, as Godot keeps user shader code.
- Alternatives: `mediump`/`RelaxedPrecision` (SPIRV-Cross drops it for MSL, and drivers may ignore it); half precision across the whole shader (the light loop, shadow and fog maths need FP32 range and precision); runtime specialisation constants (SPIR-V cannot change types through a constant); one FP16-only shader (desktop GPUs without `shaderFloat16` would lose PBR).
- Consequences: one extra pbr.frag variant in the shader library; on lavapipe the three reference scenes rendered at ultra with FP16 forced on stay inside the FP32 goldens' limits (mean FLIP 0.0028 to 0.0035, worst tile 0.0087 against limits of 0.005 and 0.01), and `tests/cli/reference_scenes.cmake` now gates that. The minimum roughness rises from 0.045 to 0.089 in the FP16 variant, so mirror-like highlights are broader. Metal stays FP32 until the MSL variant is tested on Apple hardware, and Vulkan 1.0/1.1 drivers without the feature run the FP32 shader. The speed-up has not been measured here (no phone GPU or Mali offline compiler in this sandbox).

## ADR-0136 — Probe sun occlusion

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: the distance shadowmask (ADR-0131) keeps static sun shadows on lightmapped scenery past the realtime shadow distance, and baked probes (ADR-0133) give dynamic objects the baked ambient, but a character or prop standing under a roof past the last cascade was still lit by the full sun. On phones (one short cascade) that is most of the visible distance. Unity's Shadowmask mode stores the same occlusion in the light probes: "Unity generates Shadow masks and Light Probe occlusion data for baked shadows", and dynamic objects receive "baked shadows from static GameObjects using Light Probes, beyond the Shadow Distance" ([Unity Manual, Lighting Mode: Shadowmask](https://docs.unity3d.com/2022.3/Documentation/Manual/LightMode-Mixed-Shadowmask.html)); Unity support describes probes as the way static objects shadow dynamic ones without realtime shadows, giving "soft, approximate shadowing" ([Unity support](https://support.unity.com/hc/en-us/articles/207820473-Why-don-t-my-static-objects-cast-shadows-on-dynamic-objects-such-as-characters)).
- Decision: when the bake has a shadowmask light (the first shadow-casting directional light, `settings.shadowmask`; now one `shadowmask_light` helper for texels and probes), each valid probe also traces `shadowmask_samples` rays over the sun's disc (the texel R2 sequence, `disc_visibility` shared with the shadowmask) into `LightmapProbes::sun` (0..1). Invalid probes get it from the same inverse-squared-distance 3 x 3 x 3 dilation as their SH. `LightmapProbes::sample_sun` is the CPU reference. `.vlightmap` version 4 adds flag 8 (only valid with the probe flag 4): one byte per probe after the probe block; versions 1 to 3 still load, and a version 3 header with flag 8, or flag 8 without probes, is rejected. `IrradianceVolume::set_probe_sun` (default 1) puts visibility x validity in the alpha of SH coefficient row 1, which the shader fetched but never read, so the texture size and fetch count are unchanged and hand-placed volumes stay unoccluded. `GpuAssetCache` copies it into the lightmap's probe volume when the file has a shadowmask direction. In pbr.frag `volume_irradiance` also returns `sun_vis = mix(1, c[1].a / valid, valid)`; the volume is now sampled before the sun term, and for unlightmapped pixels that value is the `baked` input of `shadow_factor` (the same distance-shadowmask path lightmapped pixels use, enabled only while the sun matches the baked direction within about 1 degree).
- Alternatives: four occlusion channels for four mixed lights as Unity does (one shadowmask light is all VICTUS bakes today); a separate occlusion texture (one more binding and fetch for the same data); per-object CPU sampling at the bounds centre like Unity's legacy probes (cheaper on the GPU, but one value for a whole object, so a character half under a roof is all or nothing).
- Consequences: dynamic objects past the realtime shadow distance are shadowed by static geometry at no extra shader fetch; shadows are as soft as the probe spacing (2 m by default), so they bleed out from under small casters, and visibility is sampled at the probe centres, so a narrow shadow between probes can be missed. Dynamic casters still need the cascades. File growth: one byte per probe (at most 16 KB). Tiers with `irradiance_volumes` off (Low) do not use the baked probes at all, so they get neither the probe ambient nor this occlusion yet.

## ADR-0135 — Congruent lightmapped copies share one unwrap and batch

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: every lightmapped renderer got its own world-space xatlas unwrap, so its GPU mesh was re-indexed with its own atlas uv stream. Instancing merges draws only when they use the same mesh, so lightmapping a scene broke the batching it relied on: the Multiplayer Arena went from 8 to 13 main-view batches and from 29 to 53 GPU buffers when its walls and four identical pillars were baked (ADR-0134). Both big engines keep one lightmap uv set per mesh and give each renderer a scale and offset into the atlas: Unity's `Renderer.lightmapScaleOffset` ("multiple Renderers can use different portions of the same lightmap", [Unity scripting API](https://docs.unity.com/en-us/engine/6000.6/script-reference/unityengine/renderer/lightmapscaleoffset)), Unreal's per-component lightmap coordinate scale and bias ([FLightMapInteraction](https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/API/Runtime/Engine/FLightMapInteraction?application_version=5.3)), with a lightmap UV channel that is "unique to each static mesh" ([Understanding Lightmapping](https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/understanding-lightmapping-in-unreal-engine)).
- Decision: `unwrap_lightmaps` (`LightmapSettings::share_uvs`, on by default) groups receivers whose meshes are identical (fingerprint, vertex and index counts) and whose transforms differ only by a rotation and translation (equal Gram matrix of the linear part within 1e-5, same handedness). Such copies are congruent, so one unwrap has the same texel density on all of them. When any group has two or more members, each group's first member is unwrapped by xatlas on its own and placed once per member, every other receiver is unwrapped together by xatlas into one more block (keeping xatlas's tight joint packing for unique meshes), and the blocks are shelf-packed (several widths tried, smallest square side kept) with a `padding` gap. Without any shared group the single xatlas pass runs exactly as before. Members' uvs then differ by a constant offset; the `.vlightmap` format is unchanged (each entry still stores its own atlas uvs). At load, `GpuAssetCache::lightmapped_mesh` hashes the source mesh (every vertex attribute and index) and reuses an already-uploaded lightmapped mesh when `render::lightmap_uv_offset` finds the new entry is the same unwrap shifted by a constant; the draw passes that shift to `SceneRenderer::submit`, which stores it in the instance data's spare `normal[1].w` / `normal[2].w` (skinned palettes use `normal[0].w`; lightmapped meshes are never skinned), and `mesh_lightmap.vert` adds it to the atlas uv. Only offsets are needed because congruent copies have the same block size; scale stays 1.
- Alternatives: object-space unwraps scaled per instance like Unity's Scale In Lightmap (shares across non-uniform scales too, but texel density then varies per copy and stretched copies get distorted texels); merging static meshes into one buffer per material like Unity's static batching (memory per copy, and LOD and culling granularity lost); per-instance uv streams in a storage buffer indexed by instance id (no vertex-format change, but needs storage-buffer reads in the vertex stage on every backend).
- Consequences: Multiplayer Arena main batches 13 to 9, GPU buffers 53 to 38, draw calls 84 to 73 (Medium, 640x360, frame 120); the GPU test draws three baked cubes in one instanced batch with pixel-identical output (worst channel difference 0). Old bakes and scenes without congruent copies are unaffected; non-uniformly stretched copies of one mesh (the arena's north and west walls) still get their own unwrap. The shelf packing of blocks wastes more atlas area than xatlas's chart packing when many groups exist, which can lower the density a scene gets at a given `max_atlas_size`.

## ADR-0134 — Templates ship with baked lighting

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: baked lighting (ADR-0122 to ADR-0133) only helps projects whose authors find Static Lighting and Bake Lighting; every new 3D project started unbaked, so the Low tier (no SSAO) showed no contact darkening under steps, pillars or crates, dynamic characters took the open-sky ambient everywhere, and nobody saw the workflow. Unity 6 removed continuous Auto Generate but keeps a "Bake On Scene Load: If Missing Lighting Data" mode that bakes a scene whose lighting data is missing, and gives new scenes a default Lighting Data Asset so they never open unlit ([Lighting window](https://docs.unity.com/en-us/engine/6000.6/manual/lighting-overview/lighting-reference/lighting-window), [Unity 6 upgrade guide](https://docs.unity.com/en-us/engine/6000.7/manual/upgrade-guides/upgrade-guide-unity6)).
- Decision: the 3D templates (3D Scene, No-Code 3D Adventure, Multiplayer Arena) mark their static scenery (ground, islands, steps, walls, pillars, brazier) as Static Lighting, and `victus new` and Studio's New Project bake the startup scene right after creating the project (`player::bake_template_lighting`: 128 samples, 3 bounces, 8 texels/m, light probes; 0.8 to 3.2 s on two cores). `victus new --no-bake` skips it; a failed bake only warns. Bake logic shared by `victus bake-lighting`, `victus new` and Studio lives in `victus/player/bake_lighting.hpp` (`bake_project_lighting`). `--mark-static` now also skips meshes moved by a Rotator, Oscillator, Moving Platform, nav agent or sequence player on the entity or a parent (`is_static_for_lighting`): before, the 3D template's rotating, bobbing Floating Gem was marked and its texels went to waste because the moved mesh's fingerprint never matched.
- Alternatives: committing pre-baked `.vlightmap` files for each template (would have to be regenerated on every template or baker change and embedded in the binary); baking lazily on first run (costs start-up time on the player, worst on phones); baking in a background job in Studio (the bake is under a few seconds, so the synchronous call keeps New Project simple).
- Consequences: new 3D projects open lit by the bake, with contact darkening on every tier and light probes for characters; perf budgets now measure the lightmapped templates (two extra textures and a probe volume). Templates' lightmaps are files in the new project, so changing the scene needs a re-bake (moved objects fall back to realtime ambient, ADR-0125). 2D, simulation and no-code 2D templates are unchanged.

## ADR-0133 — Baked light probes for dynamic objects

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: after ADR-0122/0130/0131 static scenery receives baked sky light, bounces, contact darkening and sun shadows, but everything that is not lightmapped (characters, pickups, physics props, skinned meshes) still took its ambient from the open sky: a character walking under a roof or into a building stayed as bright as outside, which breaks the image exactly where baked lighting is meant to sell it. Hand-placed `IrradianceVolume`s (ADR-0056) fix that only where an author places one, and they bake on the GPU with per-probe cube captures at run time (the probe budget is spent on the device). Every competitor bakes probes with the lightmaps: Unreal's Volumetric Lightmap is interpolated per pixel for dynamic objects ([Volumetric Lightmaps](https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/volumetric-lightmaps-in-unreal-engine)); Unity's Adaptive Probe Volumes mark probes that see back faces as invalid and keep them out of the lookup ([probe validity](https://docs.unity3d.com/6000.4/Documentation/Manual/urp/probevolumes-light-probe-validity.html), [APV panel](https://docs.unity.com/en-us/engine/6000.6/manual/lighting-overview/lighting/probevolumes/lighting-panel-reference)); Godot's LightmapGI generates probes automatically with `generate_probes_subdiv` ([LightmapGI](https://docs.godotengine.org/en/latest/classes/class_lightmapgi.html)).
- Decision: the lightmap baker (`LightmapSettings::probes`, on by default) also bakes an SH9 probe grid with the same CPU path tracer, sky, bounce count and direct-light mode as the texels (`Tracer::trace_path`, shared with `gather`; the lightmap texels are bit-identical to before). Grid: `probe_bounds`, or the scene's bounds padded by 0.25 m and at least 3 m tall; `probe_spacing` (2 m, half of it vertically so a 3 m room gets four layers rather than one in the floor and one above the furniture) with the IrradianceGrid limits (32 x 16 x 32), spacing growing when the box needs more. Each probe traces `probe_samples` (256) uniformly distributed paths (spherical Fibonacci, rotated per probe) and projects them into SH9 pre-convolved with the cosine lobe and divided by pi (the `SphericalHarmonics9` convention); in fully baked mode the bake lights' direct light is added as SH deltas. Probes where more than 25 % of first hits are back faces are invalid (Unity's validity rule). `probe_dilation` (on) then gives each invalid probe the inverse-squared-distance mean of its valid raw neighbours in the surrounding 3 x 3 x 3 block (Unity APV's probe dilation); probes with no valid neighbour stay invalid. The volume lookup renormalises over valid corners and fades to the sky by the missing weight, so without dilation a character standing next to a probe swallowed by geometry (including other non-lightmapped meshes, which are blockers in the bake) would drift back towards sky lighting: the GPU test sphere measured 0.4 valid weight before dilation. `render::bake_light_probes` bakes probes alone. `.vlightmap` version 3 adds a flagged probe block after the directional atlas (bounds, counts, then per probe one validity byte and 27 half floats; counts, validity and bounds validated; versions 1 and 2 still decode, and a version 2 header with the probe flag is rejected). `GpuAssetCache` loads the block into an `IrradianceVolume` with the lightmap; `extract_scene` binds it for every non-lightmapped surface unless a hand-placed volume contains the camera (that one is more specific), with a normal offset of min(0.25 m, 0.2 x spacing). `ExtractStats::lightmap_probes` reports it. `victus bake-lighting --probe-spacing <m> | --no-probes` and Studio's Bake Lighting report the probe count (the CLI also prints invalid and dilated counts).
- Alternatives: GPU cube-capture probes at bake time (ADR-0056 path; would not match the CPU-traced lightmap's units and costs device time); tetrahedral probe sets like Unity's legacy Light Probe Groups (adaptive, but needs a tetrahedralisation and a per-object CPU lookup); an adaptive brick grid like Unreal's Volumetric Lightmap (denser near geometry; the next step if regular-grid memory or detail becomes the limit); L1 SH (4 coefficients, cheaper, but loses the soft directionality that keeps characters from looking flat).
- Consequences: dynamic objects in baked scenes darken under cover and pick up colour bleeding with no run-time bake and no extra shader cost (the irradiance-volume path existed: 18 bilinear fetches per pixel on volumes, already paid by any scene with a volume). Memory: 32 x 16 x 32 probes is about 0.9 MB on disk and 2.4 MB of RGBA16F on the GPU at most. Limits: a regular grid (no adaptive density near geometry), no virtual offset (Unity moves invalid probes out of geometry; dilation copies neighbours, which can leak light through walls thinner than the spacing), points whose eight surrounding probes stay invalid fall back to the sky, SH9 ringing behind strong single lights in fully baked mode, no baked sun occlusion for dynamic objects beyond the realtime shadow distance (Unity's probe occlusion; follow-up), and the probes are lit by the analytic sky like the lightmap. Not measured on phones.

## ADR-0131 — Baked sun shadowmask (distance shadowmask)

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: phones render one 1024 cascade over a short shadow distance (Low tier), so static scenery past a few tens of metres loses its sun shadows and looks flat; raising the distance costs shadow-map draws and resolution on exactly the devices that can least afford it. Unity's default mixed-lighting mode on mobile is Distance Shadowmask (baked static shadows past the realtime distance); Unreal's stationary lights bake distance-field shadowmaps for the same reason.
- Decision: the lightmap baker (`LightmapSettings::shadowmask`, on by default) traces the first shadow-casting directional light's visibility per texel over the sun's disc (`sun_angular_radius`, 0.01 rad; `shadowmask_samples` 32, R2 sequence) and stores it in the atlas alpha, dilated with the irradiance; `LightmapBake::shadowmask_direction` records the light. `.vlightmap` version 2 gains a flag and the direction (files without it, including version 1, have no mask). `Environment::lightmap_shadowmask_direction` (set by scene lightmaps automatically) turns it on in pbr.frag through `Lighting.cascade_params.w`: inside the cascades the realtime map still shades every caster, the last cascade fades into the baked value instead of to 1, and beyond it lightmapped pixels take the baked visibility. The renderer only enables it while the sun points within about 1 degree of the baked direction and casts shadows, so a moved sun never shows stale shadows. Cost: one texture channel that was already fetched; no extra pass.
- Alternatives: full shadowmask (static casters skipped in the shadow map, baked shadows everywhere: cheaper still but needs per-caster static flags in the shadow pass; possible follow-up); signed-distance shadowmaps (sharper at low resolution, more complex bake); longer realtime distance on phones (the cost this avoids).
- Consequences: static sun shadows at every distance and on every tier; dynamic objects still need the realtime cascades for their own shadows, and they receive no baked shadow (no probes yet). Coverage alpha (1 / 0.5 / 0) is only kept for bakes without a mask.

## ADR-0130 — Directional lightmaps

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: ADR-0122 lightmaps store irradiance for the interpolated vertex normal only, so normal maps went flat wherever baked light replaced the ambient: bricks, tiles and terrain lost their relief in exactly the shadowed areas where baked light matters most. Unity's default is directional lightmaps (a second texture with the dominant light direction); Unreal bakes two SH coefficients per texel for the same reason; Godot's LightmapGI offers L1 SH.
- Decision: the baker (`LightmapSettings::directional`, on by default) accumulates the luminance-weighted direction of every first-bounce ray (and of the bake lights in fully-baked mode), filters it with the same edge-aware denoiser weights and dilates it with the irradiance. `LightmapBake::direction` is an RGBA8 atlas in Unity's encoding: RGB = dominant direction * 0.5 + 0.5 (length = directionality), A = the rebalancing term 0.5 * dot(n, d) + 0.5 at the baked normal (>= 0.5; 0 = no data). pbr.frag samples it at binding 11 and scales the baked irradiance by (0.5 * dot(N, d) + 0.5) / A, which is exactly 1 for the baked normal, so flat surfaces are unchanged and normal-mapped ones are re-lit. `.vlightmap` version 2 adds a flags word and the RGBA8 atlas; version 1 files still load (no direction). `GpuContext::create_lightmap_direction_texture`, `Environment::lightmap_direction`, and the scene's lightmap loads it automatically. Cost on device: one extra RGBA8 bilinear fetch per lightmapped pixel and 4 bytes per texel (half the irradiance atlas).
- Alternatives: L1 SH (three RGB coefficient sets, 3-4x the memory and fetches; better for strongly coloured directional light, kept as a later option); per-texel bent normals only (no directionality, cannot reduce to the plain bake); baking with normal maps in the tracer (resolution bound to the lightmap, aliases).
- Consequences: baked scenes keep their normal-map detail on every tier for one texture fetch. A single dominant direction cannot represent light from two opposite sides; such texels have low directionality and fall back towards the non-directional result. Fragment sampler count of pbr.frag goes from 11 to 12 (SDL_GPU allows 16).

## ADR-0127 — Device-class quality selection ("auto" tier)

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: phones ran at `rendering.mobile_quality` (Medium by default) whatever the GPU, so a Mali-G52 got the same work as an Adreno 750. Unity ships per-platform quality levels that games pick from by device; Unreal uses device profiles matched on the GPU family (`Android_Mali_G7x`, `Android_Adreno6xx`, ...). Without that, "runs on anything" means tuning for the weakest phone everywhere.
- Decision: `victus/render/device_class.hpp`. `classify_device(DeviceProfile{gpu name, mobile, RAM, compute}, fallback)` matches the GPU family in the driver's device name and maps generations to tiers: Mali T-series, G31-G68 and G3xx Low, G71-G78 and G6xx Medium, G7xx/G9xx/Immortalis High; Adreno below 620 Low, 620-649 and 710/720 Medium, 650-699, 730+ and 8xx High; PowerVR Low; Xclipse and Apple High; Intel integrated at most Medium; software rasterizers (llvmpipe, SwiftShader, WARP) Low; anything else keeps the fallback. RAM and compute only lower it: phones under 3 GB Low, under 5 GB at most Medium; desktops under 4 GB at most Medium; no compute shaders at most Medium. `rendering.quality` and `rendering.mobile_quality` accept `"auto"`, which is now the phone default (Medium when the GPU is unknown; desktop projects keep their explicit tier, High when "auto" meets an unknown GPU). `victus run --quality auto` and the player log print the decision ("Mali-G52: entry-level GPU").
- Alternatives: a startup GPU benchmark (accurate, but costs seconds on every launch and is noisy on thermally throttled phones; may be added later as a refinement); a downloaded device database like Android's Performance Tuner or Unreal's device profiles (needs a service and upkeep); RAM-only rules (misses the large GPU spread at equal RAM).
- Consequences: projects that never set `mobile_quality` now start low-end phones at Low and flagships at High instead of Medium everywhere. The table is a heuristic from public GPU generations, not measurements; it has not been checked on real phones yet (ROADMAP hardware proof).
- Amended 2026-11-20: Arm's 5th-generation names ("Mali-G1-Ultra MC12" in the Dimensity 9500, "Mali-G1-Premium", "Mali-G1-Pro"; https://www.arm.com/products/silicon-ip-multimedia) parsed as "G1" and fell to Low; Ultra and Premium now map to High and Pro to Medium. Mesa PanVK reports the flagship parts as "Immortalis-G925 MC12" without "Mali-" (Arm's own driver says "Mali-G720-Immortalis MC12"), which was unrecognised and fell back to the project default; it now maps to High. Both are covered in `victus_render_tests` "device class: phone GPU families map to tiers".

## ADR-0126 — Packed HDR scene colour by default on phones and the Low/Medium tiers

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: ADR-0050 added `SceneRendererOptions::compact_hdr` (R11G11B10F scene colour, bloom chain and TAA history: 4 bytes per pixel instead of 8) but nothing turned it on, so every phone paid RGBA16F bandwidth (audit finding A5, docs/audits/PHOTOREAL_LOW_END_AUDIT.md). Unreal's mobile renderer and Godot's Mobile renderer both default to packed or 10-bit colour targets on phones for the same reason.
- Decision: the player creates its renderer with `compact_hdr = player::compact_hdr_by_default(tier, mobile)`: on for any phone and for the Low and Medium tiers on every platform, off for High and Ultra on desktop. `PlayerOptions::compact_hdr` and `victus run --hdr compact|full|auto` override it. The choice is fixed for the run because the pipelines are built for the format; devices that cannot render to R11G11B10F fall back to RGBA16F. The render path report gains an `hdr_precision` feature (Active at RGBA16F; Reduced, reason `setting`, at R11G11B10F), and `victus run` prints `hdr: <format>`.
- Rest of the roadmap item: the depth pre-pass already runs only for SSAO, occlusion culling or an explicit `depth_prepass` (Low and Medium have none; covered by `gpu quality: tiers schedule the expected passes`). Folding FXAA into the tonemap pass was rejected: FXAA needs the tonemapped neighbourhood, so a folded pass would tonemap 5-9 HDR taps per pixel (more ALU and more HDR reads than the separate RGBA8 pass it saves).
- Consequences: Low/Medium and phone frames move half the colour bytes through the HDR chain. Mantissas drop to 6/6/5 bits, which stays within about one 8-bit level on average after tonemapping (GPU test below). Studio and the editor viewport keep RGBA16F; High/Ultra desktop output is unchanged.

## ADR-0125 — Scene lightmaps: Static Lighting flag, per-scene bake file, CLI and Studio

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: ADR-0122 gave the engine a baker and a lightmapped draw path, but no scene could use them. Unity marks renderers "Contribute GI" and stores LightingData per scene; Unreal bakes Lightmass into the level. VICTUS needs the same with no hidden state: a bake must be a plain project file that diffs, exports and fails safe.
- Decision: `MeshRendererComponent::lightmapped` ("Static Lighting" in the inspector, saved only when true) marks receivers. `victus/runtime/scene_lightmap.hpp` gathers them (`prepare_scene_lightmap`, UI thread: primitive, procedural and model LOD0 meshes, Directional/Point/Spot lights, the analytic sky of the Environment) and bakes them (`bake_prepared_lightmap`, any thread). The `.vlightmap` file is written next to the scene (`default_lightmap_path`) and referenced from `EnvironmentComponent::lightmap` (project-relative). Each entry's fingerprint mixes the mesh fingerprint with its world matrix (quantised to 1e-4), so moving, scaling or editing a mesh makes its entry stale and it falls back to realtime lighting instead of drawing wrong light. `GpuAssetCache` loads the file sandboxed to the project (absolute paths and `..` rejected), caches failures, and swaps in the lightmapped mesh at extraction; scattered instances stay unbaked. `victus bake-lighting <project> [--scene] [--samples] [--bounces] [--density] [--max-size] [--threads] [--direct] [--dry-run]` and Studio Assets > Bake Lighting (gather on the UI thread, trace on a worker, one undo step) run the same code. Export packs the referenced file.
- Alternatives: storing the atlas inside the `.vscene` JSON (bloats diffs, slows loads); per-entity lightmap files (many small reads on phones, one atlas texture is better for batching); invalidating by entity UUID only (would keep drawing old light after a move).
- Consequences: a static scene opts in per mesh and pays one texture fetch per lit pixel. Limits: the bake uses the analytic sky (no HDRI or reflection probes in the bake), constant base colour per material (no albedo textures in the bake), metals receive no diffuse bake, skinned and morphing meshes are skipped with a warning, and any transform edit needs a re-bake.

## ADR-0128 — Player frame cap at the target frame rate

- Numbering: committed as ADR-0124 in 590ffef after ADR-0124 (receiver-aware shadow culling, d2f3269) was already on main; renumbered to ADR-0128 (ADR-0126 and ADR-0127 were taken by e1dcc28 and 2589045).

**Decision.** Interactive player runs with a target frame rate (`rendering.target_fps`, or `rendering.mobile_target_fps` on phones) now sleep the rest of each frame period instead of rendering at the display's vsync rate. Dynamic resolution is fed the frame's busy time (work plus present, without the sleep), so the cap does not read as a slow frame. Fixed-frame, record and replay runs are uncapped as before.

**Why.** On a 90 Hz phone such as the Infinix Hot 10S, vsync alone lets the game try to render 90 frames a second. A Mali-G52-class GPU cannot sustain that, so it heats up, throttles and then misses even the target. Holding a steady 60 (or 30) and spending the slack sleeping is what keeps a low-end SoC cool over a long session.

**Consequences.** On a 90 Hz panel a 60 Hz cadence alternates one- and two-vsync intervals. A target of 45 or 90 paces evenly, and so does a platform frame-rate hint, which VICTUS does not set yet. Sleep granularity is about 1 ms on desktop and Android. Verified on desktop (lavapipe, 5 FPS target: 73 frames in 15 s, p50 199 ms); not yet measured on the Hot 10S.

## ADR-0122 — Baked lightmaps v1 (renderer and baker)

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: the photoreal-on-low-end track (docs/audits/PHOTOREAL_LOW_END_AUDIT.md) needs global illumination that costs one texture fetch on a phone. Unreal's mobile renderer does not run Lumen and relies on precomputed lightmaps ([Lumen technical details](https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/lumen-technical-details-in-unreal-engine), [mobile performance guidelines](https://docs.unrealengine.com/5.1/en-US/performance-guidelines-for-mobile-devices-in-unreal-engine/)); Unity ships baked lightmaps in several encodings for the same reason ([lightmap data](https://docs.unity.com/en-us/engine/6000.6/manual/lighting-overview/direct-and-indirect-lighting/lightmap-data/lightmaps-technical-information)). Lightmaps need a second, non-overlapping UV set; xatlas (MIT) generates one ([xatlas](https://github.com/jpcy/xatlas)).
- Decision: `victus/render/lightmap.hpp`. `unwrap_lightmaps` packs every receiving instance into one xatlas atlas (texel density `texels_per_unit`, automatically lowered until the atlas fits `max_atlas_size`, `padding` texels between charts; xatlas built single-threaded). `bake_lightmaps` path-traces each texel on the CPU (binned-SAH BVH over all instances, Möller–Trumbore, cosine-weighted hemisphere with R2 low-discrepancy sequences, `bounces` diffuse bounces, sun/point/spot lights with shadow rays, an analytic sky identical to `pbr.frag`'s `environment(dir, 0)` or a callback) on the job system, and stores irradiance divided by pi so the shader multiplies by albedo directly. By default only indirect light is baked (`include_direct = false`): real-time lights and shadows still draw direct light, so baked and dynamic lighting do not double count. Texels whose hemisphere rays hit more than 25 % back faces are inside geometry and rejected (leak prevention), then filled. A variance-guided à-trous filter (normal, position and luminance-variance stops, three passes) removes noise without blurring contact shadows; dilation fills `dilation` texels around charts. The atlas is RGBA16F without mips (alpha = coverage/occlusion). `encode_/decode_lightmap_file` store the atlas and per-mesh UV sets with mesh fingerprints (`VICTUSLM` v1, bounds-checked). On the GPU, `create_lightmapped_mesh` adds the UV set as vertex buffer slot 3; `mesh_lightmap.vert` passes it at location 14 to `pbr.frag`, which samples `u_lightmap` (set 2 binding 10) and replaces ambient irradiance with it and scales specular occlusion by the baked occlusion. `Environment::lightmap` turns it on; meshes without a lightmap UV set render as before.
- Alternatives: GPU baking (faster, but needs ray queries or compute BVH traversal that SDL_GPU does not expose; the CPU baker runs everywhere, including CI); light probes only (no contact shadows on static geometry); RGBM/dLDR 8-bit encoding (smaller; planned for v2 together with ASTC); per-mesh atlases (more textures and draw-state changes).
- Consequences: static scenes get bounce light and contact darkening for one texture fetch per pixel. v1 bakes LOD0 only, ignores skinned meshes, stores non-directional irradiance (normal maps get no baked direction), has no shadowmask, no RGBM/ASTC encoding and no light probes for dynamic objects, and is not wired into scenes yet (next step: per-scene `.vlightmap`, `victus bake-lighting`).

## ADR-0123 — Terrain layers: height blending, derivative bump and cavity from layer alpha

- Numbering: committed as ADR-0121 in 4213be3 at the same time as the Orion and Nova ADR-0121; renumbered to ADR-0123 so each number is unique (ADR-0122 is baked lightmaps v1).
- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20)
- Context: the built-in terrain surface cross-faded four layer textures by splat weight and shaded them with the flat vertex normal. Photoscanned ground (mud, leaf litter, moss, rock) then looks like a printed carpet: transitions are soft smears instead of stones and leaves poking through mud, and the micro relief that sells a photo texture at grazing angles disappears. Shipped engines solve this with height-based layer blending (e.g. Unreal/Unity HDRP "height blend" terrain layers) plus a normal map per layer, but a normal map per layer doubles texture fetches and memory, which the Mali-G52-class phones VICTUS targets cannot spare, and the terrain mesh has no tangents.
- Decision: the alpha channel of each terrain layer texture is an optional height map (1 = top, 0 = crevice). The shader (`terrain_surface_code`, engine/terrain/src/terrain.cpp):
  - weights each active layer by `w * exp2(12 (h - hmax))` and renormalises, so the tallest material wins narrow transitions;
  - bumps the shading normal from screen-space derivatives of the blended height using the surface-gradient form of Mikkelsen, "Bump Mapping Unparametrized Surfaces on the GPU" (2010), amplitude 0.04 m times the material normal scale; no tangents, no extra fetches;
  - darkens crevices (`occlusion = 1 - 0.6 (1-h)^2`) and makes them up to 25% smoother (moisture);
  - samples with `textureGrad`, gradients taken once in uniform control flow, so the per-layer branches stay legal and mips stay correct.
- Compatibility: an opaque layer texture (alpha 1 everywhere) or an untextured layer gives exactly the old result (equal heights, zero height derivatives, cavity 0), so existing projects render unchanged.
- Consequences: one shader, the same four fetches, a handful of ALU ops and four derivative instructions per terrain pixel. Limits: derivative bump is per 2x2 quad, so very fine height detail aliases slightly at a distance (mips soften it); no parallax/occlusion mapping; height is per layer texture only (no separate height texture slot); BC1/ETC-style formats without alpha drop the height, so use BC7/ASTC for height-carrying layers.

## ADR-0120 — Behaviours: no-code gameplay components

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: every template's gameplay was a Luau script, so a beginner had to write code before anything moved. The tools beginners pick most attach ready-made behaviours to objects instead: GDevelop's platformer, top-down, health and other behaviours ([GDevelop behaviors](https://wiki.gdevelop.io/gdevelop5/behaviors/all-behaviors/)) and Construct 3's Platform, Bullet and related behaviors ([Construct 3 behavior reference](https://www.construct.net/en/make-games/manuals/construct-3/behavior-reference)). Both make a playable character from one checkbox-like step and expose every tuning value as a property.
- Decision: fifteen reflected components in `engine/script` (category "Behaviours"): PlatformerMovement, TopDownMovement, CameraFollow, Health, Hazard, Collectible, Spawner, Patrol, Chase, Projectile, Shooter, Lifetime, Checkpoint, Goal and GameRules. They run inside the script host's fixed and variable updates (after scripts in fixed update, before physics events in update), so they work wherever scripts run. Movement drives the existing character controllers (added automatically when missing); touch is a bounds overlap; randomness is a seeded SplitMix generator; restarts reload the scene document captured at start. They emit named events into the same `on_event` channel scripts use. A "nocode" project template builds a complete platformer from them.
- Alternatives: shipping Luau scripts as a behaviour library (still code to read, slower, and no typed inspector fields); a separate gameplay module (would duplicate the event and scene-restart plumbing that lives in the script host); an event-sheet editor first (a much larger tool; behaviours are what event sheets act on anyway).
- Consequences: a beginner can make a game from the Add Component menu. Touch uses bounds rather than exact shapes, projectiles ignore walls, score does not survive scene loads and Patrol does not detect walls (KNOWN_LIMITATIONS). No 3D no-code template yet.

## ADR-0119 — Volumetric fog on blended surfaces, particles and precipitation

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: the volumetric fog froxel grid (ADR-0098) was composited once after blended geometry by the opaque depth, so a glass pane 2 m away in front of a wall 10 m away received the full 10 m of fog, and particles and precipitation (drawn after the composite) received none. Frostbite's unified volumetric rendering integrates scattering and transmittance into a froxel volume, and also brings particles into the same participating-media model ([Hillaire 2015, Physically Based and Unified Volumetric Rendering in Frostbite](https://www.ea.com/frostbite/news/physically-based-unified-volumetric-rendering-in-frostbite)); a volume of integrated values can be looked up per fragment at any depth, which is what blended surfaces need.
- Decision: when fog or clouds are active, the forward pass always splits into opaque and blended passes, and the order becomes opaque, decals, water, clouds composite, fog inject/integrate, fog composite, blended geometry, GPU particles, particles, precipitation (the graph runs passes in declaration order). `pbr.frag` (and therefore surface-shader variants), `particle.frag` and `precipitation.frag` (drops and splashes) bind the integrated grid as a fragment storage buffer (SPIR-V after the samplers, MSL after the uniform buffers) and fetch it trilinearly at the fragment's view depth with the same slice mapping as `fog_apply.frag`: alpha-blended output becomes `colour x transmittance + in-scattering`, additive output `colour x transmittance`; opaque draws are untouched (`MaterialUniforms::blend`). The grid size and far distance ride in `LightingUniforms::volumetric` and the particle and precipitation blocks; w = 0 turns the lookup off, with a placeholder buffer bound.
- Alternatives: a separate fogged composite per transparent layer (needs per-layer depth); sampling a 3D texture instead of the storage buffer (needs the integrate pass to write a storage texture; the buffer works on every backend today); per-vertex fog lookup for particles (cheaper, but wrong across large billboards).
- Consequences: transparent and particle fog matches their distance; clouds now sit behind blended geometry instead of being drawn over it. One lookup per fragment adds a few storage reads to blended pixels. Water stays fogged by its depth (it writes depth before the composite).

## ADR-0118 — Rain splashes from the occlusion map

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: after ADR-0113/0114/0117 rain fell, was stopped by roofs and rippled puddles, but nothing showed where drops hit the ground. Lagarde's rain write-up for Remember Me draws splashes as short-lived billboards placed on the first surface below using the same top-down rain depth map that occludes the drops, so no CPU collision is needed ([Water drop 2b, dynamic rain and its effects](https://seblagarde.wordpress.com/2013/01/03/water-drop-2b-dynamic-rain-and-its-effects/)).
- Decision: a second precipitation pipeline (`splash.vert` + `precipitation.frag` shape 2, alpha-blended, depth-tested, no depth writes) draws `round(drops x 0.25 x Precipitation::splashes)` splash quads after the drops, only for rain and only when the rain occlusion map exists. Each splash hashes its index and its life cycle (0.3 s, phase-offset per splash) into a point on a disc of `precipitation_radius` around the camera, reads the occlusion depth there in the vertex shader (point-sampled) and reconstructs the landing point with the inverse rain matrix; texels with nothing below (cleared depth) and points outside the map collapse the quad. The quad is a camera-facing crown about 0.12 m across (varied per splash), standing on the hit point, growing and fading over its life. `SceneStats::precipitation_splashes`; `EnvironmentComponent::precipitation_splashes` (0..1).
- Alternatives: GPU particle collision with the depth buffer (only on-screen surfaces, needs the compute path); CPU raycasts per splash (costly, physics-dependent); spawning secondary droplet particles (more convincing; later via sub-emitters).
- Consequences: splashes land on whatever the occlusion map sees, including roofs, within its range; they follow the map's resolution (a texel can straddle an edge); no droplets, no splash sound hooks, and splashes do not wet or ripple surfaces beyond what ADR-0117 does.

## ADR-0117 — Puddles and rain ripples

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: after ADR-0113/0114 wet ground was uniformly wet and still, with no standing water. Lagarde's wet-surface series separates wet porous surfaces from puddles of standing water, where the surface below is fully soaked and the water top is a flat, smooth dielectric with rain ripples on it ([Water drop 2b, dynamic rain and its effects](https://seblagarde.wordpress.com/2013/01/03/water-drop-2b-dynamic-rain-and-its-effects/); [Water drop 3b](https://seblagarde.wordpress.com/2013/04/14/water-drop-3b-physically-based-wet-surfaces/)). Remember Me drew ripples as an animated normal layer on puddles, driven by rain intensity.
- Decision: `Environment::puddles` (0..1) and `puddle_size` (m), `Precipitation::ripples` (0..1). In `pbr.frag` (so surface-shader variants too), flat upward faces (geometric normal y above 0.85..0.95) hold puddles where two octaves of 2D value noise over world xz / size exceed `1 - puddles` (soft 0.08 edge); inside, wetness is 1, the shading normal is pulled to the geometric normal and roughness to 0.045. While rain is drawn, ripples perturb the normal by the slope of expanding rings: one ring per 0.35 m cell at a random centre and phase, radius growing to 0.9 cells over 1 / 1.3 s, a cosine profile 0.12 cells wide fading with age squared; full strength on puddles, a quarter of the wetness elsewhere, scaled by rain intensity x `ripples`, faded out where a pixel covers more than a third of a cell (derivative taken in uniform control flow) to avoid sparkle. Puddles and ripples are masked by the rain occlusion map (ADR-0114). `LightingUniforms` gains one vector; `SceneStats::surface_puddles` / `rain_ripples`; `EnvironmentComponent` gains `puddles`, `puddle_size`, `precipitation_ripples`.
- Alternatives: a ripple normal-map flipbook (needs an authored texture and a sampler slot); puddle masks from vertex colour or a painted texture (more control; can come later as a material input); real reflections in puddles (needs SSR or planar reflections, which do not exist yet).
- Consequences: puddles reflect the sky and probes but not nearby geometry; puddle placement is procedural (not following low points or a mask); ripple cost is 9 ring evaluations per flat wet pixel; no splashes yet.

## ADR-0116 — Distance fog on particles, trails and precipitation

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: `Environment` distance fog (linear, exponential, exponential squared) was applied in `pbr.frag` and `water.frag` only, so billboards, ribbon trails and rain or snow stayed at full colour inside a fog bank and popped out against fogged geometry. Unity's built-in particle shaders apply the same fog as surfaces (`UNITY_APPLY_FOG`), and fade additive particles towards black rather than the fog colour (`UNITY_APPLY_FOG_COLOR(..., fixed4(0,0,0,0))`), because their light is added on top of an already fogged background ([Unity built-in shaders](https://github.com/TwoTailsGames/Unity-Built-in-Shaders/blob/master/DefaultResourcesExtra/Particle%20Add.shader)).
- Decision: `particle.frag` (CPU and GPU billboards and ribbons) and `precipitation.frag` evaluate the same fog function as `pbr.frag` per fragment. The distance is the range along the pixel's ray, rebuilt from the view depth they already have and `1 / projection scale` (`view depth x |(ndc.x / P00, ndc.y / P11, 1)|`), so no vertex shader changes. Alpha blending mixes the colour towards the fog colour; additive blending scales the colour by `1 - fog`. The fog uniforms ride in the particle and precipitation fragment blocks.
- Alternatives: per-vertex fog (cheaper, wrong across large billboards); composing volumetric fog before transparents and sampling the froxel grid in every transparent shader (the correct fix for volumetric fog, a larger change; still open).
- Consequences: effects match fogged surfaces. Volumetric fog is still composited once after all transparents by the opaque depth; 2D sprites are not fogged (2D scenes have no distance fog).

## ADR-0115 — Whole-volume 3D texture uploads and checked uploads in the SDL backend

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: `CommandBuffer::upload_texture` always uploaded a region of depth 1 and `Device::read_texture` read one slice, so a `Tex3D` texture only received its first slice (ADR-0111 chose procedural value noise over baked Perlin-Worley volumes for this reason). Uploads also trusted the caller: a short pixel span made the GPU copy read past the staging data, and a bad mip or layer went straight to the driver. SDL_GPU describes a 3D copy with `SDL_GPUTextureRegion::d` (depth) and uses `layer` only for arrays and cubes ([SDL_GPUTextureRegion](https://wiki.libsdl.org/SDL3/SDL_GPUTextureRegion)).
- Decision: for `Tex3D`, uploads cover the whole mip volume (`d = max(1, depth >> mip)`, data x fastest, then y, then z) and readback returns every slice; layers must be 0. Every upload checks the mip and layer against the texture and, for uncompressed formats, that the data covers the region; failures are logged and skipped (no upload counted). Transfer-buffer creation and mapping failures are logged instead of dereferencing null.
- Alternatives: per-slice uploads through `layer` (does not match SDL's 3D addressing); returning a `Result` from `upload_texture` (an API change across every caller; logging matches `upload_buffer`).
- Consequences: baked 3D textures (noise volumes, LUTs) now work on the SDL backend; uploading one slice of a volume at a time is not supported.

## ADR-0114 — Rain occlusion map: no rain and no wetness under roofs

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: ADR-0113 rain fell through roofs and wetness darkened sheltered floors, so indoor areas needed precipitation turned off by hand. The standard fix is a depth map rendered from above along the rain direction: Tatarchuk's ToyShop renders "rain occlusion" from the light's view ([SIGGRAPH 2006 slides](https://www.advances.realtimerendering.com/s2006/Tatarchuk-Rain.pdf)), and Lagarde's wet-surface notes mask wetness with an occlusion test from above ([Water drop 3b](https://seblagarde.wordpress.com/2013/04/14/water-drop-3b-physically-based-wet-surfaces/)).
- Decision: when precipitation is drawn, `SceneRenderer` renders a `rain_occlusion` depth pass with the shadow pipelines: an orthographic view along the fall velocity (wind included), `ceil(1.5 x radius)` metres either side of the camera, from `kRainOcclusionHeight` (200 m) above to `2 x half + 10` m below, texel-snapped so it does not swim. Casters are the opaque draws that intersect its frustum (`SceneStats::precipitation_occluders`). `precipitation.frag` fades each drop by a comparison sample of the map (10 cm bias), and `pbr.frag` (and surface-shader variants) multiplies `wetness` by the same test offset 5 cm along the geometric normal (`set 2 binding 9`; fragment storage buffers move to 10 and 11). `RenderSettings::precipitation_occlusion` is the map size (Low 256, Medium 512, High and Ultra 1024; 0 off, otherwise clamped to 64..4096); `SceneStats::precipitation_occlusion` reports it.
- Alternatives: a ray march through the scene (no structure to march), per-volume "indoor" boxes (manual), and reusing the sun's cascades (wrong direction and range).
- Consequences: one extra depth pass sized by the tier. Points outside the map (far away) count as exposed; wetness under roofs is only masked while precipitation is falling (a scene set wet without rain stays uniformly wet); alpha-tested foliage casts as opaque; edges are hard (one comparison tap).

## ADR-0113 — Weather: procedural rain and snow around the camera, wet surfaces

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: the roadmap's environment item still lacked weather. Rainfall in games is drawn as many small streaks close to the camera rather than simulated world-wide; Tatarchuk and Isidoro's ToyShop system ([Eurographics NPH 2006](https://diglib.eg.org/server/api/core/bitstreams/7f49ec3f-1a4e-4b9f-b1bf-80c0a9f11787/content), [SIGGRAPH 2006 slides](https://www.advances.realtimerendering.com/s2006/Tatarchuk-Rain.pdf)) combines rainfall near the viewer with artist-set direction and speed, and wet materials. Lagarde's wet-surface model ([Water drop 3b](https://seblagarde.wordpress.com/2013/04/14/water-drop-3b-physically-based-wet-surfaces/)) darkens porous dielectrics by up to 0.2x and makes them glossier (`factor = lerp(1, 0.2, porosity)`, diffuse `*= lerp(1, factor, wet)`, gloss towards 1 by `lerp(1, factor, 0.5 wet)`), and notes smooth materials and metals do not darken.
- Decision:
  - `render::Precipitation` on `Environment` (type None/Rain/Snow, intensity, wind, radius, fall speed, size, streak, colour, opacity, brightness) drawn by a new `precipitation` pass after particles and before TAA: `precipitation.vert` places `intensity x RenderSettings::max_precipitation` drops procedurally from a hash of the instance index in a cube of `2 x radius` that wraps around the camera (`eye + mod(p + v t - eye, size) - size/2`), so drops are fixed in world space, deterministic in renderer time and need no simulation state or buffers. Rain is a quad stretched along velocity x streak time, snow a camera-facing disc with a small flutter; drops fade towards the cube faces and right in front of the camera; drops narrower than one pixel are widened to one pixel with alpha scaled down by the same ratio (no shimmering). `precipitation.frag` tests against the sampled scene depth with a 0.1 m soft fade. Drops are lit by the average sky radiance plus a share of the sun.
  - `Environment::wetness` (0..1) in `pbr.frag` (so surface-shader variants too): porosity = `saturate((roughness - 0.2) / 0.6) x (1 - metallic)` (our mapping, the post uses porosity textures), Lagarde's darkening and roughness scale, plus a water film on up-facing faces pulling roughness towards 0.08. Applied before the coverage layer, so snow (the existing coverage settings) sits on top.
  - `RenderSettings::precipitation` and `max_precipitation` (Low 2048, Medium 8192, High 16384, Ultra 32768; sanitised to `kMaxPrecipitation` 65536); `SceneStats::precipitation_drops` and `surface_wetness`; render-path feature `precipitation` (Off by scene / capture mode / tier or setting / missing pipeline, Reduced below the Ultra budget). `EnvironmentComponent` gains `precipitation` (enum), `precipitation_intensity`, `_wind`, `_radius`, `_size`, `_speed`, `_color`, `_opacity`, `_brightness` and `wetness`.
- Alternatives: GPU particle emitters attached to the camera (ADR-0097) — needs an entity and per-particle state, and drops follow the camera unless the emitter is in world space; an image-space rain layer as in ToyShop — cheap but no parallax or depth occlusion; a full ripple/puddle system (Lagarde 2b) — deferred.
- Consequences: one instanced draw with no buffers. Not occluded by roofs (no top-down rain occlusion map), no splashes, ripples or puddles, wetness is uniform (no masks, no drying over time), drops are not fogged or cloud-shadowed, and cost is unmeasured on real GPUs.

## ADR-0112 — Cloud shadows on lit surfaces

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: ADR-0111 added a ray-marched cloud layer but the ground stayed fully sunlit under overcast skies. Production engines darken the sun on surfaces under clouds: Unreal's volumetric cloud component renders a cloud shadow map ([Unreal Engine docs](https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/volumetric-cloud-component-properties-in-unreal-engine)), Frostbite projects a cloud transmittance texture ([Frostbite](https://www.ea.com/frostbite/news/physically-based-sky-atmosphere-and-cloud-rendering)), and Horizon Zero Dawn reuses its density field for shadowing ([Guerrilla](https://www.guerrilla-games.com/read/the-real-time-volumetric-cloudscapes-of-horizon-zero-dawn)).
- Decision: evaluate cloud shadow analytically in `pbr.frag`: intersect the sun ray from the shaded point with one height inside the layer (30% up), sample the same value-noise shape (two octaves, same wind offset, coverage threshold and scale as `clouds.frag`), turn density into optical depth over half the layer thickness divided by the sun's elevation (clamped at 0.2), and multiply the sun term by `mix(1, exp(-od), shadow_strength)`. Parameters ride in three new `LightingUniforms` vectors; `VolumetricClouds::shadow_strength` (default 1) and `EnvironmentComponent::cloud_shadows` (0..1) control it; `SceneStats::cloud_shadows` reports it.
- Alternatives: a cloud shadow map rendered per frame (extra pass, texture, more correct; deferred until a shadow-texture budget exists); applying it through CSM (would not reach beyond cascade range).
- Consequences: no new pass or texture; the shape matches the visible clouds at one height, not the full integrated column; water, 2D lit sprites, fog sun shafts and probe captures are not cloud-shadowed yet.

## ADR-0111 — Volumetric clouds: reduced-resolution ray-marched cloud layer

- Status: accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: skies were a gradient, an HDR map or the physical atmosphere (ADR-0074) with no clouds, the first item of the "weather and clouds" gap. Production engines ray march a cloud layer with procedural density: Horizon Zero Dawn's cloudscapes (Schneider & Vos, SIGGRAPH 2015, https://www.guerrilla-games.com/read/the-real-time-volumetric-cloudscapes-of-horizon-zero-dawn) threshold a low-frequency shape noise by coverage and a height gradient and erode the edges with high-frequency detail, lit by a short light march with Beer's law; Frostbite (Hillaire, "Physically Based Sky, Atmosphere and Cloud Rendering in Frostbite", SIGGRAPH 2016, https://www.ea.com/frostbite/news/physically-based-sky-atmosphere-and-cloud-rendering) adds energy-conserving integration and a multiple-scattering approximation; Unreal's Volumetric Cloud component (https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/volumetric-cloud-component-properties-in-unreal-engine) exposes layer bottom/height, tracing sample counts and a view sample scale per quality level.
- Decision: `Environment::clouds` (`VolumetricClouds`: coverage, extinction per metre, bottom/top heights, shape and detail sizes, detail erosion, wind in m/s, anisotropy, sun and ambient multipliers, march distance) describes one flat layer. A fullscreen fragment pass `clouds.frag` runs at render resolution / `RenderSettings::cloud_downsample` into an RGBA16F target after the blended geometry and before volumetric fog is composited. Per pixel it intersects the view ray with the slab, clipped at the farthest scene depth of the pixel's footprint and at `distance`, takes `cloud_steps` samples with an interleaved-gradient-noise offset that changes per frame under TAA, and evaluates density as a 4-octave value-noise fBm (rotated octaves, no textures) thresholded by `1 - coverage × profile(height)` and eroded by a 2-octave detail fBm. Each sample with density marches `cloud_light_steps` towards the sun (no detail noise) and lights the sample with three Wrenninge-style multiple-scattering octaves (weights 1, 0.5, 0.25; extinction 1, 0.3, 0.09; phase anisotropy halved each octave) of a dual-lobe Henyey-Greenstein phase, plus the mean sky radiance (the same SH/gradient estimate the fog uses) darkened towards the base; in-scattering is integrated per step as `T × S × (1 − e^{−σΔ})` (albedo 1). The density fades out over the last 40% of `distance`. `clouds_apply.frag` composites the target with premultiplied alpha at full resolution and leaves pixels whose geometry is nearer than the ray's entry into the layer untouched, so silhouettes in front of the clouds stay sharp; ground seen from above the layer is covered. Tiers: Low off, Medium 1/4 resolution / 32 / 4 steps, High 1/2 / 48 / 6, Ultra 1/2 / 64 / 8. Capture mode (probe bakes) skips clouds. `SceneStats::volumetric_clouds/cloud_width/cloud_height/cloud_steps` and a `volumetric_clouds` render-path feature (scene / capture_mode / quality_tier / setting / unsupported, Reduced below Ultra) report it. `EnvironmentComponent` exposes `clouds`, `cloud_coverage`, `cloud_density`, `cloud_bottom`, `cloud_thickness`, `cloud_size` (detail = 0.15 × size), `cloud_detail`, `cloud_wind`, `cloud_anisotropy`, `cloud_sun`, `cloud_ambient` and `cloud_distance`. Wind drifts the shapes by `wind × SceneRenderer time`, wrapped every 200 km.
- Alternatives: baked tileable Perlin-Worley 3D textures as in HZD (cheaper per sample and more billowy, but needs 3D texture uploads, which the SDL backend's `upload_texture` does not handle yet; revisit with a noise bake); temporal reprojection of the cloud target with 1/16 of the pixels per frame (much cheaper, but needs a history buffer and motion handling — TAA currently averages the jitter); a spherical planet shell (horizon curvature; the flat slab with a distance fade is enough for ground-level and flight views up to tens of km).
- Consequences: scenes get animated cumulus to overcast layers at 1/4–1/2 resolution. Clouds cast no shadows and are not part of the baked physical-sky IBL or reflection probes; they are composited before particles and over blended surfaces by opaque depth (as volumetric fog is); value noise gives softer, less billowy shapes than Perlin-Worley; cost scales with steps × light steps per cloud pixel and is not yet measured on target hardware.

## ADR-0110 — GPU particle trails recorded and built on the GPU

- Status: accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: GPU emitters (ADR-0097) had no trails, so comets, tracers and magic bolts had to stay on the CPU path (ADR-0085) and its 65 536-particle cap, uploading six vertices per segment every frame. Engines that ship GPU trails keep the history on the GPU: Unity VFX Graph's strips store a fixed number of particles per strip in a ring and render them as a quad strip; Niagara's GPU ribbons build the ribbon from per-particle data in compute and draw it indirectly. The CPU path already defines the behaviour (distance-sampled points, lifetime expiry, width/alpha taper, trails-only).
- Decision: the same emitter fields drive both paths. The renderer gives each trailed GPU emitter a ring of `points` × vec4 (simulation-space position, age) per slot; the slot's count, oldest index and the point count it was recorded with live in the free `meta.yzw` of the 64-byte GPU particle, so no extra per-slot state is needed and a new point count restarts every history (as on the CPU) without dropping particles. `particle_sim.comp` records the birth point and the pre-aged point in the spawn dispatch and one point per frame in the update dispatch (after collision), with the CPU rule: append when moved ≥ `trail_min_distance` (dropping the oldest when full), expire points older than `trail_lifetime`. The update dispatch then writes the slot's strip — (points + 1) edge-vertex pairs, tail to head plus the live head, in `ribbon.vert`'s 48-byte layout with the CPU's width, colour, tangent and v — and collapses unused pairs to zero width on the last point (zero-area triangles), so a static index buffer (two triangles per segment) built once per capacity draws every strip with one `draw_indexed` and no CPU work per frame. The ribbon batch is drawn before the emitter's heads; `trail_billboards` off draws only the ribbons. Memory is bounded by `SceneRenderer::kMaxGpuTrailSegments` (2^18 slots × points, about 38 MB of vertices and 6 MB of indices at the cap): larger emitters get fewer points per particle and none below two; the render-path report marks `gpu_particles` Reduced (budget) and `SceneStats::gpu_particle_trails_reduced` counts them. Bounds grow by the trail's half width (trail points lie inside the existing travel bounds).
- Alternatives: a separate compute pass that compacts live strips and draws indirectly (less vertex work for sparse emitters, but needs indirect-argument plumbing and a second dispatch; revisit if vertex cost shows up in profiles); a vertex shader that reads the rings straight from storage buffers (no ribbon buffer, but a new pipeline layout with vertex-stage storage and duplicated colour/size logic in the vertex stage); a CPU copy of GPU positions (readback latency, defeats the GPU path).
- Consequences: GPU emitters can carry trails at GPU particle counts (e.g. 16 384 particles × 16 points). Vertex work is proportional to capacity × points even when few particles live; ribbons of one emitter are not sorted against each other (same as the CPU path); trails die with their particles and are 3D only like every GPU emitter; local-space trails are stored in emitter space and follow the entity.

## ADR-0109 — Per-tier effect budgets for decals, water and CPU particles

- Status: accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: decals, water and particles used fixed per-frame caps (1024 decals, 16 water surfaces, no particle cap) on every tier, so a Low device paid the Ultra cost for effects and the render-path report (ADR-0105) could only say `budget`. Unreal's Niagara scales effects per quality bucket with effect-type scalability (max distance, max instances, spawn count scale), and with a significance handler keeps the N most significant instances and culls the rest.
- Decision: `RenderSettings` gains `max_decals` (Low 128, Medium 256, High 512, Ultra 1024), `max_water_surfaces` (Low 4, Medium 8, High and Ultra 16) and `max_particles` (CPU billboards plus ribbon vertices drawn per frame: Low 16384, Medium 65536, High 262144, Ultra 1048576), clamped by `sanitized()` to the renderer's hard caps. Distance is the significance: decals and water keep the nearest, as before. Particle batches are kept whole from the nearest until the next batch would go over; when the nearest batch alone is over it is cut to the budget (ribbon batches are never cut), so a budget always draws something. GPU emitters are not counted, since their cost is their capacity. `RenderPathFacts` gains `particle_batches_in_view`, `particles_in_view` and `particle_budget`, and the decals, water and particles features name the cause of a cut: `quality_tier` when the budget equals the tier preset, `setting` when it was lowered by hand, and `budget` when even the Ultra reference could not hold everything.
- Consequences: Low and Medium devices draw fewer effects in dense scenes, and the report and the viewport's "below ultra" line say why. Dropping a whole far batch can pop when the camera moves; there is no distance fade or per-emitter significance yet, and the simulation still runs for culled emitters (spawn-count scaling would belong in the particle module).

## ADR-0108 — Particle sub-emitters (birth and death events)

- Status: accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: fireworks, impact sparks and smoke puffs left by embers need one emitter to spawn particles where another emitter's particles end. Unity's Sub Emitters module (Birth, Collision, Death, Trigger, Manual) and Godot's `sub_emitter` with `SUB_EMITTER_AT_END` / `AT_COLLISION` both reference a second emitter and spawn at the parent particle's position.
- Decision: `ParticleEmitterComponent` gains `sub_emitter` (entity reference, remapped by prefabs like every UUID field), `sub_emitter_trigger` (death, birth), `sub_emitter_count` and `sub_emitter_inherit_velocity`. During a CPU emitter's update each death (at the position the particle would reach in its last step) or birth queues an event with its world position and scaled velocity, at most 4096 per update. After the parallel update the system delivers events serially in work order to the target's queue (also at most 4096), so results are bit-identical with or without jobs. On its next update the target spawns `count` particles per event after its own spawns, within its `max_particles`: its own shape sample is moved to the event position and oriented by the target transform, its own speed is added to the inherited velocity, and local-space targets store the result relative to themselves. GPU targets turn the events into spawn records at the start of the step. Targets spawn whether or not they are emitting, like `emit()`, so a target with rate 0 acts only as a sub-emitter. Chains work; a self-reference, a missing entity or an entity without an emitter drops the events, counted in `ParticleStats::sub_emitter_dropped`. GPU emitters cannot be sources, because their particle positions live only on the GPU.
- Consequences: two-stage effects need no script. The target spawns one update after the source event, and collision events are not available (CPU emitters do not collide, GPU emitters cannot be sources).

## ADR-0107 — GPU depth sorting of alpha-blended GPU particle emitters

- Status: accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: GPU emitters (ADR-0097) drew their instance buffer in slot order. With alpha blending the result depends on draw order, so far particles could cover near ones and the order changed as slots were recycled. Only additive emitters looked correct.
- Decision: a compute shader `particle_sort.comp` runs a bitonic sort of (key, slot) pairs after the simulation, for every visible alpha-material GPU emitter outside capture mode. The key is `0xFFFFFFFF - floatBits(distance² to the eye)` (non-negative floats sort like unsigned integers), so the sort ascends into far-to-near order; dead slots and the padding up to `n = max(256, bit_ceil(capacity))` get the largest key and end up last. Steps with k ≤ 256 run in shared memory in one dispatch, larger merge steps use one global dispatch per j ≥ 256 followed by one shared-memory dispatch for j = 128..1, and a gather pass writes the instances in sorted order into a second buffer that the draw reads. Each step is its own compute pass, because SDL_GPU does not synchronise dispatches inside one compute pass. Pass count is 3 + Σ_{k=512..n}(log2 k − 7); 1024 slots need 8 passes. Additive emitters are not sorted (order-independent). `SceneStats::gpu_particle_sorted_emitters` / `gpu_particle_sort_passes`, `SceneRenderer::read_gpu_particle_draw` and the render-path detail (", N depth-sorted") expose it. Bitonic sorting was chosen over a radix sort because its fixed, data-independent network needs no atomics or prefix sums, and Unity's VFX Graph likewise turns on GPU sorting by default for alpha and premultiplied outputs.
- Consequences: alpha-blended GPU smoke and mist draw back to front within an emitter; cost is O(n log² n) per emitter per view each frame. Separate emitters are still ordered only by batch distance, and particles do not interleave with other transparent geometry. Sorting uses distance to the eye, not view depth.

## ADR-0106 — Script Debugger in Studio play mode (nested frame loop)

- Status: accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: the Luau source debugger (ADR-0099) only ran in the headless game behind `victus debug-adapter`. Studio play runs scripts inside the UI frame on the UI thread, so a breakpoint there would freeze the window. Moving the game loop to its own thread would touch every play-mode system (physics, audio, animation, networking, the viewport's reads of the play scene) and was not justified for this step.
- Decision: keep the game on the UI thread and stop it with a nested frame loop. `HostOptions::debugger` is on for Studio play. The stop handler installed by `Editor::apply_debugger_to_play()` ends the UI frame that was being built (`FramePump::suspend`, ImGui::EndFrame), runs whole Studio frames through the application's `FramePump::frame` until the user picks Continue / Step Over / Step Into / Step Out, then begins a new UI frame (`FramePump::resume`) so the interrupted frame can finish. The handler runs from `tick_play`, which `Editor::frame()` calls before any panel draws, so the interrupted frame holds no open windows. While stopped, `Editor::frame()` does not tick play, autosave, poll assets or advance previews; the menu bar, toolbar and all panels except the Script Debugger draw inside `ImGui::BeginDisabled`, and shortcuts are off. Stop while stopped is deferred: the call continues without further stops and play stops when `tick_play` returns. Closing the window ends the stop the same way. Opening or closing a project is refused while stopped. `victus_editor --frames N` never waits at a breakpoint for more than N frames. Without a frame pump (headless embedding) stops are counted and never block. The new Script Debugger panel (Window menu, Scripting and Debugging layouts) shows a read-only source view with a clickable breakpoint gutter and the current line, the call stack, locals and upvalues with lazily expanded tables, watches and an evaluate box for the selected frame, and the breakpoint list with conditions and log messages. Breakpoints and the break-on-error choice live in `.victus/breakpoints.json` per project. This follows the in-editor model of Defold's Lua debugger (gutter breakpoints, the game halts at a breakpoint while the editor stays live) and the stopped → stack → scopes → variables flow of the Debug Adapter Protocol, reusing the same `DebugStop` API as the DAP server.
- Consequences: scripts can be debugged where they are written, with the viewport showing the frozen frame. The whole game stops, including physics and audio, as in the headless debugger. The game cannot be stepped one frame at a time from a stop, and the source view is read-only. A game loop on its own thread is still the route to attaching to a running player.

## ADR-0105 — Render-path report for decals, water, particles and probes; water pass lifetime fix

- Status: accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: the rendering-path report (ADR-0103) did not cover decals, water, particles or reflection probes. These are the passes most likely to be silently missing from a frame: culled, over a fixed budget, never captured, or needing compute.
- Decision: `RenderPathFacts` gains submitted / in-view / drawn counts and budgets for decals (max 1024 nearest) and water (max 16 nearest), CPU particle batches, particles and ribbon vertices, GPU emitters submitted versus simulated, and reflection probes requested / not captured / over budget (max 64) / used, plus draws inside a probe box. `SceneRenderer::add_reflection_probe` now counts rejected probes instead of dropping them silently. `describe_render_path` adds five features: decals, water, particles, gpu_particles and reflection_probes. Their reasons are `scene`, `not_needed` (outside the view), `budget`, and `unsupported` (GPU emitters without compute are not drawn). No tier reason is used, because no tier preset scales these passes today.
