A third reference scene `effects.vscene` (water pool, two decals, seeded CPU sparks with trails, GPU mist, a probe around a chrome sphere) is added to `victus_reference_scenes`, which requires all five features to be active.
- Bug fixed: the `water_scene_copy` and `water` execute lambdas captured the block-local `water_scene` render-graph handle by reference. The graph runs them after that block ends, so the handle was read from a dead stack slot. In Debug the `std::vector::at` range check threw on the first frame with water (index 2771602000 of 14). Both lambdas now capture the handle by value. A scan of the other block-local `RGTexture`s in `render()` found no other by-reference use.
- Consequences: a missing effect can now be traced to a reason. The effects scene is pixel-identical across runs on lavapipe (FLIP 0), including GPU particles. Per-tier scaling of these passes, and its reporting, is still open.

## ADR-0104 — Deterministic perceptual reference scenes

- Status: accepted (2026-11-19)
- Context: the compare harness (ADR-0102) and render-path report (ADR-0103) had nothing fixed to measure. The perceptual-equivalence handoff (Slice 0) asks for deterministic exterior and interior reference scenes, with software-renderer correctness recorded separately from a high-end hardware reference.
- Decision: a generated sample project `samples/reference_scenes` (`tools/generate_reference.py`, uuid5 names, so regenerating gives identical files) with `exterior.vscene` and `interior.vscene`. Each has a fixed camera, no scripts/physics/particles, and one `Rotator` object for a temporal signal. Exterior covers the sun cascades, physical sky, height + volumetric fog, a 6-step roughness ramp on dielectric and metal spheres, 13 posts with 4 cm rails, stairs and a wall, an emissive bar. Interior covers a closed room with only local light: a shadowed point light, a shadowed spot aimed so the box and cylinder cast onto the back wall, an emissive panel, a FogVolume, a glossy floor. Goldens are lavapipe captures at ultra, 320x180, frames 15 and 30, in `golden/lavapipe_ultra_320x180/`. The CTest `victus_reference_scenes` (label gpu, skipped without `VICTUS_REQUIRE_GPU=1`) compares fresh captures with `--max-flip 0.005 --max-tile-flip 0.01 --min-tile-ssim 0.99 --max-temporal 0.005`, requires the scene's key path features to be `active` (exterior: sun_shadows, volumetric_fog, bloom, ssao, taa; interior: local_lights, local_shadows, volumetric_fog, bloom, ssao), and logs low-vs-ultra FLIP without gating. `VICTUS_UPDATE_GOLDENS=1` rewrites the goldens. Ground truth approved by hand and replaced on purpose follows Unreal's screenshot comparison workflow.
- Tolerances were measured, not guessed. The same driver reproduces the goldens exactly (FLIP 0). Exterior with bloom off gives mean 0.025, worst tile 0.043. Interior with spot shadows off gives mean 0.0013, worst tile 0.019, tile SSIM 0.966, and the limits reject it (exit 4). Low quality gives mean 0.118 (exterior) and 0.215 (interior). A mean-only limit would have missed the spot-shadow regression, which is why worst-tile limits matter.
- Consequences: renderer changes now have a fixed visual regression check. The goldens assume the same rasteriser, so another Mesa or a hardware GPU needs regenerated goldens. There is no high-end reference capture yet, and no per-tier tolerance.

## ADR-0103 — Rendering-path diagnostics with reason codes (accepted 2026-11-19)

**Context.** Slice 1 of the perceptual-equivalence handoff asks for reason codes and output-quality metadata for the existing LOD, culling, render scale, shadows and effects, with honest unavailable states for costs the platform does not expose. `SceneStats` counted work but did not say why a feature was missing: SSAO off because of the tier, a setting override, AO intensity 0 in the environment, or a missing depth pre-pass all looked the same. Once `victus compare` (ADR-0102) reports a difference, the next question is which decision caused it.

**Decision.**
- `victus/render/render_path.hpp`: `RenderPathFacts` (what the renderer saw and decided, filled in `SceneRenderer::render` at the existing decision points, no new policy) and the pure `describe_render_path(settings, facts)` producing a `RenderPathReport`: 18 features (render scale, TAA, temporal/spatial upscale, FXAA, sun shadows, local lights, local shadows, irradiance volume, SSAO, bloom, auto exposure, volumetric fog, LODs, occlusion culling, instancing, motion vectors), each `active`, `reduced` or `off` with a reason (`quality_tier`, `setting`, `scene`, `not_needed`, `replaced`, `requires`, `capture_mode`, `unsupported`, `budget`, `no_history`) and a detail with the output quality (cascades and resolution, SSAO samples and resolution, froxel grid, LOD error, lights kept of visible, atlas tiles).
- The reference is the Ultra preset: cheaper parameters are `reduced`. The reason is `quality_tier` when the value matches the active tier's preset, else `setting`, so user overrides are visible.
- GPU execution cost is reported as unavailable: SDL_GPU exposes no timestamp queries (libsdl-org/SDL#11696, open, milestone 3.6). CPU per-pass cost stays with the profiler (`--profile`, `--trace`).
- Surfaces: `SceneRenderer::path_report()`, `PlayerReport::render_path`, `victus run --render-report F.json` (prints a table and writes JSON), and a "below ultra: ..." line in the editor viewport overlay that names non-tier reasons.

**Alternatives.** A global quality manager deciding features (rejected by the handoff: extend owning systems); free-text log lines (rejected: not machine-readable for CI); estimating GPU time from draw/triangle counts (rejected: not a measurement; the handoff asks for honest unavailable states); Vulkan timestamp queries behind SDL_GPU's back (rejected: SDL_GPU does not expose the VkDevice or command buffers).

**Consequences.** CI and users can see exactly why a frame differs from the Ultra path; the CLI compare test checks low reports SSAO `off/quality_tier` and ultra `active/none` on a real frame. New decision points in the renderer must also fill `RenderPathFacts`, or the report goes stale for that feature.

**Sources.** Perceptual-equivalence handoff Slice 1 (docs/handoff/VICTUS_PERCEPTUAL_EQUIVALENCE_RENDERING.md); SDL GPU query API issue (https://github.com/libsdl-org/SDL/issues/11696); the SDL3 GPU chapter of the Linux graphics stack book (https://github.com/turbofish-pk/linux-graphics-stack-book/blob/main/chapters/part-18-rendering-abstractions/ch81-sdl3-gpu.md).

## ADR-0102 — Perceptual capture/compare harness: FLIP, SSIM, worst tile, temporal error (accepted 2026-11-19)

**Context.** The perceptual-equivalence goal (docs/handoff/VICTUS_PERCEPTUAL_EQUIVALENCE_RENDERING.md, Slice 1) needs a reproducible way to tell whether a cheaper rendering path still looks like the reference. VICTUS only had `assets::image_psnr`, and PSNR tracks pixel error, not visibility. A single average can also hide one broken object in an otherwise correct frame, and a per-frame score cannot see flicker. While building the harness, the first comparison of two identical fixed-frame runs showed they were not pixel-identical: the player never set `SceneRenderer::set_time`, so surface-shader time followed the wall clock.

**Decision.**
- `victus/render/image_compare.hpp`: `compare_images` reports mean, p95 and max LDR-FLIP (NVIDIA's `FLIP.h`, vendored at d3ab02a, BSD-3-Clause, sRGB8 decoded to linear, default 67 pixels per degree), mean SSIM on luma (Wang et al. 2004: 11-tap Gaussian σ 1.5, K1 0.01, K2 0.03, L 255, interior pixels only, as in scikit-image), PSNR, the fraction of pixels with FLIP above 0.1, and the same metrics pooled over ~64 px tiles with the worst tile's rectangle. `compare_sequences` adds a temporal error: the mean |Δtest − Δref| of linear luminance between consecutive frames, worst pair. `flip_heatmap` renders the error map in FLIP's magma map. `check_limits` turns `CompareLimits` into readable failures.
- `victus run --capture-dir DIR --capture-every N` writes every Nth frame of a fixed-frame run as `frame_NNNNN.png`; `victus compare <ref> <test>` takes two PNGs or two folders with matching names, prints per-frame and sequence metrics, writes `--heatmap` and `--json`, and exits 4 when outside `--max-flip`, `--max-tile-flip`, `--min-ssim`, `--min-tile-ssim` or `--max-temporal`.
- The player sets surface-shader time from game time (`sim.time().time`), so animated materials pause with the game and fixed-frame runs repeat exactly.
- No pass/fail thresholds are claimed for quality tiers: they need reference scenes and hardware baselines.

**Alternatives.** PSNR/MSE only (rejected: not perceptual); full SSIM/MS-SSIM only (kept SSIM as a structural second opinion; FLIP models viewing distance and colour, which SSIM on luma does not); HDR-FLIP on pre-tonemap buffers (deferred: the player captures display-referred LDR frames); writing FLIP from the paper (rejected: the reference implementation is small, header-only and permissively licensed); an image-diff library such as pixelmatch (rejected: anti-aliasing heuristics, not perceptual).

**Consequences.** Rendering changes can now be gated on image and sequence tolerances with localized evidence. `victus_cli_render_compare` (GPU) checks that two runs match exactly and that low vs ultra exceeds a tight worst-tile limit with exit 4. Comparison is single-threaded CPU code (FLIP compiled -O2 even in Debug; ~0.7 s per 640×360 frame pair on the CI VM).

**Sources.** FLIP paper and project (https://research.nvidia.com/publication/flip, https://research.nvidia.com/sites/default/files/node/3260/FLIP_Paper.pdf); reference code (https://github.com/NVlabs/flip); SSIM (Wang et al. 2004, https://docs.rs/crate/ssim/0.1.0/source/image-quality-assessment.pdf); scikit-image SSIM (https://github.com/scikit-image/scikit-image/blob/v0.18.0/skimage/metrics/_structural_similarity.py); MS-SSIM (https://www.cns.nyu.edu/pub/lcv/wang03b.pdf).

## ADR-0101 — Editor safe mode after unfinished project openings (accepted 2026-11-19)

**Context.** ROADMAP 6l asks for a safe mode that opens a project after repeated startup failures without running editor scripts or restoring a known-bad workspace or scene. Before this, a project whose startup scene, scripts, assets or autosave crashed the Studio crashed it again on every opening; the only way out was editing files by hand. Godot's recovery mode is the reference: when the editor crashes while opening a project it offers to reopen it in recovery mode, which disables tool scripts, editor plugins, GDExtension addons, automatic scene restoring and running the project, and it can also be started with `--recovery-mode`.

**Decision.**
- Detection: every `Editor::open_project` writes `<project>/.victus/editor_startup.json` with the number of openings in a row that did not finish (`unfinished`, atomic write). The record is removed after `EditorOptions::startup_frames` frames (120, about 2 s), when the project closes or when the Studio quits normally; a crash, hang or kill leaves it. A torn record counts as one. When the count reaches `safe_mode_after` (2; 0 = never) the project opens in safe mode. The count is per project, so one broken project does not affect others, and the safe opening is recorded too, so a project that still crashes in safe mode stays in it.
- Also on request: `victus_editor --safe-mode` (every project this run), launcher Recent > right-click > Open in Safe Mode, Help > Reopen in Safe Mode.
- Safe mode skips everything that runs project content automatically: the startup scene (an empty scene opens instead), the edit-time script host (Inspector script properties), asset re-import, thumbnails, the background project check, autosave recovery offers and autosave writes (copies stay on disk untouched for a normal start) and play mode. Opening scenes, editing, saving and Check Project still work. The editor has no native plugin loading (only the player, `simulate` and `serve` load plugins), so there is nothing extra to skip there.
- A card lists what was skipped and why, with Restart Normally (disabled while there are unsaved changes), Report a problem and Hide; the window title and status bar say Safe Mode. Restart Normally closes and reopens the project with everything on.

**Why not restart the process.** Reopening in-process is enough because nothing that safe mode skipped has run, and it keeps the user's window and layout. The workspace layout is a per-user ImGui file loaded before any project opens, so it is not part of the per-project failure; resetting it remains available through Window > Layout.

**Consequences.** A project that crashes the Studio twice while opening opens in safe mode the third time, with a clear explanation and a one-click way back. Tests: `victus_editor_tests -tc="studio safe mode*"` (2 cases, 71 assertions: record and removal, two unfinished openings switch it on, each skipped item, the untouched autosave, play refused, manual scene opening, Restart Normally, Open in Safe Mode, closing, torn record, `--safe-mode`, `safe_mode_after = 0`). Limits in KNOWN_LIMITATIONS: crashes before a project opens are not counted, a hang that the user waits out still counts after 120 frames as finished, and there is no per-item choice of what to skip.

**Sources.** Godot project manager, recovery mode (https://docs.godotengine.org/en/stable/tutorials/editor/project_manager.html); Godot command line `--recovery-mode` (https://docs.godotengine.org/en/latest/tutorials/editor/command_line_tutorial.html).

## ADR-0100 — Versioned format migrators, per-component versions and `victus migrate` (accepted 2026-11-19)

**Context.** Every VICTUS document carried a `version` field, but readers only compared it with the build's version: an older file could never be upgraded, a renamed component became an unknown component, and a field change inside a component had nowhere to go. ROADMAP 6l asks for explicit versioned migrators with tests for migration, backup, unknown-field preservation and malformed input. Shipped engines version data at two levels: Unreal stores a custom version per system in each package (`FCustomVersionContainer`, `FCustomVersion`) so each serializer upgrades its own data, and Unity keeps renamed fields and types readable with `FormerlySerializedAs` and `MovedFrom`.

**Decision.**
- `victus/core/migration.hpp`: a `migration::Format` is a name, a current version and a chain of `Step{from, summary, apply}` (one step per version). `upgrade(doc)` reads `version` (missing, non-integer or zero is a ParseError; newer than the build is VersionMismatch, "written by a newer VICTUS; this build reads versions 1 to N"), runs the steps in order on a copy and replaces the caller's document only when every step succeeded, so a failed migration changes nothing. It returns a `Report` (from, to, the steps applied). Chains with a gap are rejected when built.
- Formats: `victus.scene`, `victus.prefab`, `victus.project` and `victus.world` load through their `*_format()`. All are still at version 1, so the chains are empty until a format changes; the next format change adds one step instead of new branches in the reader.
- Per-component versions (Unreal's custom-version idea): `SerializerRegistry::set_version(name, version, steps)` and `add_former_name(name, former)` (Unity's `FormerlySerializedAs` for components). Saved components carry `"$version"` when their version is above 1. `scene::migrate_components` renames former names (the current name wins if both are present), runs the component's steps and records one note per change; loading strips `$version` before the component's own reader runs. Data from a newer component version is not read: it stays an unknown component and is saved back unchanged.
- Scenes and prefabs keep the notes (`Scene::migrations()`, `Prefab::migrations`). The first save of a migrated file keeps the original next to it as `<file>.premigration` (`fs::keep_premigration_copy`, written once and never replaced), in addition to the 2 verified generations of ADR-0082.
- `player::migrate_project(dir, write)` scans `victus.project`, `.vscene`, `.vprefab` and `.vworld` files (skipping dot folders, `build` and `node_modules`). Project and world files are upgraded as raw JSON so fields this build does not know survive; scenes and prefabs go through their loaders. `victus migrate <project> [--write]` prints what would change and exits 3 when files need migrating, 1 when a file is unreadable or newer, 0 otherwise; with `--write` it saves them.

**Why not migrate silently on every load and save.** Loading does migrate in memory, but writing is explicit (a save in the editor or `victus migrate --write`), so a project opened by mistake in a newer build is not rewritten, and the original is always kept once.

**Consequences.** Format and component changes now have a tested upgrade path. Tests: `victus_core_tests -tc="migration*"` (chain order, report, newer/missing/invalid versions, failing step leaves the document unchanged), `victus_scene_tests -tc="scene migration*"` (component steps, former names, `$version` written, newer data round-trips, `.premigration` kept once, prefabs) and `victus_export_tests -tc="migrate*"` (report, newer file error, dot folder skipped, write, rescan clean). Limits are in KNOWN_LIMITATIONS: no real document migrations exist yet, prefab overrides are not migrated per field, and tile set, sprite, controller, sequence and graph files keep their own version checks.

**Sources.** Unreal Engine `FCustomVersionContainer` (https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/API/Runtime/Core/FCustomVersionContainer?lang=en-US) and `FCustomVersion` (https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/API/Runtime/Core/FCustomVersion?lang=en-US); Unity `FormerlySerializedAsAttribute` (https://docs.unity.com/en-us/engine/6000.0/script-reference/unityengine/serialization/formerlyserializedasattribute); Unity forum discussion of `MovedFrom` for serialized references (https://discussions.unity.com/t/formerlyserializedasattribute-for-serializereference/878027).

## ADR-0099 — Luau source debugger with breakpoints, stepping and inspection over the Debug Adapter Protocol (accepted 2026-11-19)

**Context.** Scripts could only be debugged with `log.info` and the error overlay; the only debugger was the visual-graph trace view (ADR-0060). Every comparable engine ships source-level script debugging (Godot's GDScript debugger, Unity/Unreal through IDE debuggers, Roblox Studio for Luau). Luau has the needed VM support: `lua_breakpoint` patches bytecode with BREAK instructions that cost nothing until hit, `lua_singlestep` with a `debugstep` callback steps line by line, and `lua_getinfo`/`lua_getlocal`/`lua_getupvalue` inspect frames when chunks are compiled with debug level 2 (Luau C API; Lute's debugger and luau-debugger use the same design).

**Decision.**
- `HostOptions::debugger` (off by default) compiles scripts with debug level 2 and installs the `debugbreak`, `debugstep` and error callbacks. Without it nothing changes and `set_breakpoints` reports why breakpoints cannot verify.
- Engine API in `victus/script/debugger.hpp`: `ScriptHost::set_debug_handler(handler, output)`, `set_breakpoints(path, {line, condition, hit_condition, log_message})` returning resolved `BreakpointState`s (moved to the next line with code, re-applied on every load and hot reload), `set_break_on_error`, thread-safe `request_pause`, `debug_stats`. The handler gets a `DebugStop` (reason, message, frames, locals, upvalues, table children, `evaluate(expr, frame)`, `set_variable`) and returns Continue / StepIn / StepOver / StepOut. The game thread blocks inside the handler; the time spent there is added back to the script call budget.
- Breakpoints are BREAK patches only. Stepping and pause turn on single-step mode at the next top-level call and turn it off when the step ends: measured in the Debug build, single-stepping with a trivial callback made a 3M-iteration loop 4× slower (107 ms vs 439 ms). A step requested from a breakpoint in a call that was not single-stepping patches breakpoints onto every line of every module until it completes; a step that leaves its call stops at the next script line anywhere. Line events are de-duplicated per stack depth.
- Conditions and expressions are compiled with `luau_load` in an environment table of the frame's upvalues and locals, falling back to the function's environment; the `return expr` form is tried first. Hit conditions accept `N`, `==N`, `>N`, `>=N`, `<N`, `<=N` and `%N`. Logpoints interpolate `{expr}`. Uncaught errors stop through the `pcall` error handler while the stack is still intact.
- `victus/script/dap.hpp`: `DapSession` implements the Debug Adapter Protocol requests (initialize, setBreakpoints, setExceptionBreakpoints, configurationDone, threads, stackTrace, scopes, variables, evaluate, setVariable, continue, next, stepIn, stepOut, pause, disconnect, terminate) over a `ScriptHost`; transport and `launch` stay with the application. `breakpoint` events report breakpoints that verify once their script loads. `DapDecoder`/`dap_encode` implement the Content-Length framing.
- `victus debug-adapter` serves DAP on stdio. It keeps the original stdout for the protocol and points file descriptor 1 at stderr, so console logging cannot corrupt the stream. A reader thread feeds a queue. `launch {project, scene, frames, stopOnEntry, saves, noDebug}` opens the headless game of `victus simulate` with the debugger on, then runs it at 60 Hz once `configurationDone` arrives. Script logs and warnings go to the debug console. `tools/vscode-victus` contributes the `victus` debug type to VS Code. Other DAP clients (nvim-dap, Zed, Helix) can launch the same command.
- `ChildProcess::read_bytes` reads exact byte counts for framed protocols.

**Why not a socket server inside the player or editor first.** The protocol logic is reusable and transport-free. Stdio lets editors start it with no port management, and the headless game is deterministic and testable in CI. The Studio's in-process play runs scripts inside the ImGui frame, so blocking there would freeze the editor. That integration needs a game thread or a re-entrant UI loop (see KNOWN_LIMITATIONS).

**Consequences.** Line, conditional and hit-count breakpoints, logpoints, stop on uncaught errors, step over/in/out, pause, call stacks, locals/upvalues/table inspection, watch/hover evaluation and editing locals all work for `.luau` scripts. Tests: `victus_script_tests -tc="script debugger*"` (8 cases, 340 assertions) and `victus_cli_debug_adapter` (end to end over stdio on the 3D template). With the debugger off there is no cost. With it on, breakpoints cost nothing until hit and stepping is slow only while active.

**Sources.** Lute debugger architecture (https://lute.luau.org/cli/debug/ARCHITECTURE.html), Luau C API (https://luau.org/api/), Luau performance notes on breakpoints (https://luau.org/performance/), luau-debugger (https://github.com/sssooonnnggg/luau-debugger), godot-luau-script debugger discussion (https://github.com/Fumohouse/godot-luau-script/discussions/2), Debug Adapter Protocol overview (https://microsoft.github.io/debug-adapter-protocol/overview).

## ADR-0098 — Froxel volumetric fog with shadowed sun, clustered local lights, fog volumes and temporal reprojection (accepted 2026-11-19)

**Context.** VICTUS fog was a per-material distance term (Slice 35.1): it could not be lit, cast no light shafts, ignored shadows and local lights and had no local density. Shipped engines (Assassin's Creed 4, Frostbite, Unreal, Godot 4, HDRP, The Last of Us Part II) all use the camera-aligned froxel volume of Wronski (SIGGRAPH 2014, https://bartwronski.com/wp-content/uploads/2014/08/bwronski_volumetric_fog_siggraph2014.pdf): light and density are evaluated once per froxel by a compute pass, a second pass marches each froxel column from the camera accumulating in-scattering and extinction, and shading looks the result up at the pixel's depth. Hillaire (Frostbite, SIGGRAPH 2015, https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2015/) made it physically based (scattering/extinction coefficients, phase function) and replaced the naive accumulation by an analytic step that conserves energy at any slice thickness. Godot's fog volumes (https://godotengine.org/article/fog-volumes-arrive-in-godot-4/) add authored density shapes to the same grid.

**Decision.**
- Grid: render-resolution tiles of `RenderSettings::volumetric_fog_tile` pixels (Medium/High 16, Ultra 8; capped at 160×90 columns) × `volumetric_fog_slices` (48 / 64 / 96), view depth distributed quadratically (depth = far·t²) up to `VolumetricFog::distance` (8–1000 m, default 80). Low disables it. Stored as vec4 storage buffers rather than 3D textures because the RHI has no read-write storage-texture binding yet; the composite does its own trilinear fetch (8 loads).
- `fog_inject.comp` (4×4×4 groups): per froxel, at a per-frame R1-jittered depth inside its slice, extinction = exponential height fog (uniform below `height`, exp(−falloff·(y − height)) above) plus up to `kMaxFogVolumes` (8, nearest visible first) oriented boxes with soft edges; scattering = extinction × albedo. In-scattered radiance = sun irradiance × Henyey-Greenstein phase (g = `anisotropy`) × the cascade shadow (one hardware-PCF tap, same atlas and matrices as the forward pass, faded at the shadow distance) + the mean sky radiance (SH band 0 of the environment map, or the analytic zenith/horizon/ground average; the isotropic phase integrates a uniform sky to its radiance) + every clustered local light of the froxel's cluster with the forward pass's attenuation, spot cone and local shadow atlas. Each term has a scale (`sun_intensity`, `ambient_intensity`, `local_light_intensity`). The froxel centre is reprojected with last frame's unjittered view-projection and camera; inside the old grid the result is blended with a trilinear history fetch (`temporal`, default 0.9; 0 = no jitter and no history, deterministic). History is dropped on grid or distance changes, `reset_history()`, frames without fog and capture mode.
- `fog_integrate.comp` (8×8 columns): front to back, slice thickness × 1/cos of the column ray, per slice T·(S − S·e^(−σt·ds))/σt and T ·= e^(−σt·ds) (Hillaire 2015); stores (scattered light, transmittance) at each slice's far edge.
- `fog_apply.frag`: full-screen pass after the blended geometry and water, before particles and TAA; linearises the depth (sky = far end of the grid), interpolates the grid in x/y and in √(depth/far)·slices, and writes (scattering, 1 − T) with premultiplied-alpha blending, i.e. scene·T + scattering.
- API: `Environment::volumetric_fog` (`VolumetricFog`), `SceneRenderer::add_fog_volume(FogVolume)`, `SceneStats::volumetric_fog/fog_froxels_x/fog_froxels_y/fog_slices/fog_volumes`; `EnvironmentComponent::volumetric_*` (reflected, saved) and the new `FogVolumeComponent` ("FogVolume", Rendering; size scaled by the entity transform, density, albedo, edge fade, visible), extracted only while the environment's fog is on; editor Create menu / command palette entry "Fog Volume" with its box as selection bounds. The light and cluster buffers gain compute-read usage.

**Why not ray marching per pixel, or 3D textures now.** Per-pixel marching costs O(pixels × steps × lights) and needs heavy denoising; the froxel grid is O(froxels × lights) independent of resolution (an 80×45×64 grid is 230 k cells against 2 M pixels at 1080p). 3D storage textures would give hardware trilinear filtering in the composite and in particles/transparent shading; they need an RHI extension (read-write storage textures in compute passes), recorded as the follow-up.

**Consequences.** Lit, shadowed fog with light shafts, local light halos and authored fog boxes on Medium and above; a uniform medium matches the analytic solution (tests: within 3/255 of L·T + A·(1−T) at T = e^−1 and e^−2, and with the distance limit). Limits: blended (transparent) surfaces, water and particles are fogged only by what lies behind them (the composite runs after them and uses the opaque depth); no volumetric shadows from the fog itself (self-shadowing) or from particles; fog volumes are boxes only (no ellipsoids or density textures, no noise animation); froxel resolution blurs shadow edges; one tap of cascade and local shadow per froxel (temporal jitter softens it); no GPU timing measured (lavapipe only).

## ADR-0097 — GPU particle simulation with CPU-owned emission, curl-noise turbulence and depth-buffer collision (accepted 2026-11-19)

**Context.** VICTUS particles (Slice 13, ADR-0084/0085) were simulated and billboarded on the CPU and uploaded every frame: fine for a few thousand sparks, too slow and too bandwidth-heavy for the hundreds of thousands of particles of rain, embers, magic or debris that current engines draw. Shipped approaches: Wicked Engine (and the Frostbite/Unity VFX Graph lineage) keep the whole particle life cycle on the GPU with dead/alive index lists and atomic counters, emit in a compute pass, simulate in another and collide against the depth buffer (https://wickedengine.net/2017/11/gpu-based-particle-simulation/); Unreal Niagara splits emitter logic (spawn rate, bursts, lifetime of the emitter) on the CPU from per-particle scripts on the GPU. Turbulence in film and games is commonly curl noise (Bridson, Hourihan, Nordenstam 2007), divergence-free so particles swirl without bunching.

**Decision.**
- Split like Niagara, with the slot bookkeeping kept exact on the CPU: `ParticleEmitterComponent::gpu` switches an emitter to the GPU path. The particle system keeps the existing emission schedule (rate, bursts, cycles, `emit()`), a min-heap of death times and a free-slot stack, and produces spawn records — the initial particle state from the same PCG32 stream and shape sampling as the CPU path, spawn times spread over the step, emitter translation interpolated from the previous to the current transform. Live counts, `status()`, `finished` and conservative bounds (spawn boxes grown by speed·life + ½·(|g| + turbulence)·life² and the drawn extent) therefore stay exact and deterministic with no GPU readback, and the slot of a particle is known before the GPU sees it, so no atomics or append buffers are needed.
- The renderer owns the GPU state per emitter key (64-byte particle per slot plus a 48-byte instance per slot) and runs `particle_sim.comp` in the render graph after depth is complete and before the particle pass: a spawn dispatch writes new records into their slots, pre-aged to the frame clock, then an update dispatch integrates every live slot over the frame and writes its billboard (dead slots collapse to size 0). The particle pass draws `capacity` instances straight from that buffer (vertex + compute-write usage). A new emitter instance, a `reset()` or a capacity change zeroes the state; records already seen (by spawn index) are skipped, dead ones dropped, so a renderer that misses frames catches up. States not submitted for 300 frames are released. GPU emitters are always simulated (also when off screen) but only drawn when their bounds are visible; capture-mode renders (probes) draw without simulating.
- Integration mirrors a C++ reference kernel (`particles::gpu_integrate`/`gpu_billboard`) exactly: substeps of at most 1/30 s (up to 8), semi-implicit Euler, exponential drag, spin, size/colour over life, flipbook frames and velocity-stretched aiming identical to the CPU path. Tests compare GPU readback against the reference.
- Turbulence (both paths): a sum of two octaves of a periodic curl field, each component a cosine of one other coordinate (so ∇·a = 0 exactly), the second octave rotated by an orthonormal matrix and at 2.13× frequency; `turbulence` (m/s²), `turbulence_frequency` (cycles/m), `turbulence_speed` (phase rad/s, wrapped in double so it stays precise for days). CPU emitters with `turbulence = 0` are unchanged.
- Depth collision (`collision`, world-space GPU emitters): a particle projected behind the visible surface by at most `collision_thickness` is moved back onto it along the view ray, the surface normal is rebuilt from neighbouring depth texels, and the normal velocity is reflected with `bounce` and the tangential velocity reduced by `friction`. Off-screen or hidden particles do not collide (the standard screen-space limitation).
- Limits up to `kMaxGpuParticles` (2^20) slots per emitter; `max_particles` now accepts that range (CPU emitters still clamp at 65 536).

**Why not a fully GPU-driven life cycle (dead/alive lists, indirect draws).** It removes the CPU spawn cost but makes alive counts, `finished`, bounds and script queries asynchronous (readback latency) and the result order-dependent across GPUs; the CPU cost here is a few hundred nanoseconds per spawned particle and nothing per live particle. Revisit when spawn rates in the millions per second are needed or for GPU-side events (sub-emitters on collision).

**Consequences.** Simulation and billboarding move off the CPU and the per-frame upload shrinks to the new spawns; GPU emitters stay deterministic and inspectable. Limits: 3D only (the 2D sprite path skips GPU emitters), no trails, alpha particles are not depth sorted (use additive or low-alpha), collision is screen-space, multiple renderers (e.g. two viewports) each simulate their own copy and collide against their own camera's depth.

## ADR-0096 — Geometry occlusion with Steam Audio material transmission, reverb zones and underwater filtering (accepted 2026-11-19)

**Context.** VICTUS audio (miniaudio, ADR-0083 voice management) attenuated spatial sources by distance only: a sound behind a concrete wall was as loud and as bright as one in the open, every room shared one hand-set reverb, and a camera under the new water (ADR-0095) heard the world dry. Shipped approaches: Steam Audio (Valve) traces the direct path against scene geometry and applies per-band transmission from acoustic materials (3 bands centred at 400 Hz, 2.5 kHz, 15 kHz; `IPL_MATERIAL` presets in `core/src/core/phonon.h`), with volumetric occlusion that traces several rays across a source radius; Wwise/FMOD-style engines add authored reverb zones/rooms that crossfade the reverb as the listener moves. The roadmap's dependency gate asked for a small VICTUS-owned ray/query path to be compared before adopting Steam Audio.

**Decision.**
- Geometry: `PhysicsWorld::raycast_all` (Jolt `AllHitCollisionCollector<CastRayCollector>`, sorted, triggers excluded). Audio does not link physics: a new `gameplay::SegmentQuery` scene resource (`gameplay/queries.hpp`) is installed by `physics::bind(scene, world)` and cleared with `bind(scene, nullptr)`; any future provider (navmesh, a baked acoustic mesh) can install the same hook.
- Materials: `AcousticMaterialComponent` ("AcousticMaterial", Audio) with Steam Audio's eleven presets (energy transmission per band, copied from phonon.h) or Custom values; lookup walks to the nearest ancestor with the component, otherwise Generic.
- Occlusion (opt-in `AudioSource.occlusion`, optional `occlusion_radius` for 5 rays: centre plus ±u/±v perpendicular to the listener→source direction): per ray the band energies of all surfaces in between multiply; rays average (unblocked rays count 1). Hits on the listener's or source's own hierarchy are ignored. Amplitudes are √energy. The low band sets the voice gain; the mid/low ratio sets a per-voice first-order low-pass (cutoff solved in closed form so |H(2.5k)|/|H(400)| equals the ratio, clamped 150 Hz–20 kHz) inserted between the sound and its bus group only while needed, with the filter's own 400 Hz loss compensated. Gain and log-cutoff are smoothed (τ = `occlusion_smoothing`, 80 ms); the first trace snaps. A per-update ray budget (`max_occlusion_rays`, 64) is shared round-robin, never-traced voices first.
- Reverb zones: `ReverbZoneComponent` ("ReverbZone", Audio) — an oriented, scaled box with `fade` margin, `decay`, `wet` (send for Sfx/Ambient/Voice), optional `lowpass`, `priority` (then smallest box wins). The zone's weight and character are smoothed (τ = `zone_smoothing`, 0.25 s) and layered on the user's bus settings: send = max(user, w·wet), decay = lerp(user, zone, w), low-pass = min of user/zone/water in log-frequency. Music and Ui are untouched; `bus_*` getters keep returning user values; with no zone the effective values equal the user's exactly.
- Underwater: when `gameplay::query_water` reports the listener below a surface, Sfx/Ambient/Voice blend to `underwater_cutoff` (600 Hz) by smoothstep over the first 0.3 m (`underwater_filter` toggles it).
- Fix found on the way: miniaudio's spatializer bypasses master volume and panning entirely for `ma_attenuation_model_none`, so spatial sources with Attenuation None ignored their volume; VICTUS now runs None as linear with zero rolloff.
- API: `AudioEngine::update(scene, dt)` (the PostUpdate system passes the frame dt), `acoustics()` (zone, weights, effective decay/sends/low-pass, rays, occluded count), `occlusion(entity)`; Luau `audio.acoustics()` and `entity:audio_occlusion()`.

**Why not Steam Audio now.** Its direct-path model is what is adopted (same material data, same volumetric idea), but the SDK adds a ~10 MB binary per platform, its own scene/BVH that must mirror physics, and threading/licensing integration; the VICTUS path reuses Jolt's broadphase and stays a few hundred lines. HRTF, reflections/propagation and baked probes remain the reason to revisit it.

**Consequences.** Occluded sources lose level and brightness by material, partial cover gives in-between results, rooms change the reverb smoothly. Cost is bounded by the ray budget (one Jolt ray per voice per update by default). Limits: transmission only (no diffraction around edges, no reflections/propagation, no portals), thickness is not modelled (each surface counts once), the zone low-pass and reverb are global (one listener), first-order tilt filter. Sources: Steam Audio phonon.h (https://github.com/ValveSoftware/steam-audio/blob/480dd64f513cc8a6437e7d5b9eb0d3f1d30c2fac/core/src/core/phonon.h), Jolt `CastRay` with collectors (https://jrouwe.github.io/JoltPhysicsDocs/5.6.0/class_narrow_phase_query.html), miniaudio spatializer source (`ma_spatializer_process_pcm_frames`).

## ADR-0038 — Blended materials as a sorted sub-pass of the forward pass (accepted 2026-11-17)
- Context: water, glass, decals and glows need alpha and additive blending; the forward renderer only had opaque meshes and separate particle passes.
- Decision: `Material::blend` (`.vmat` `"blend": "opaque" | "alpha" | "additive"`, inheritable by instances). Blended draws skip the depth pre-pass and shadow casting, are sorted far to near by bounds centre, and are drawn at the end of the forward pass (same lighting, fog and coverage uniforms) with depth test on and depth write off. Pipelines are created on first use per blend × skinned × double-sided, and surface shaders get the same variants. Surface shaders also read the interpolated vertex colour (`SurfaceInput::vertex_color`), so meshes can carry per-vertex data for them.
- Consequences: straight (non-premultiplied) alpha; per-object, not per-triangle, sorting — intersecting blended meshes can still order wrongly; no order-independent transparency, refraction or screen-space reflections; blended draws do not appear in SSAO or the depth used by soft particles.


### ADR-0121: Orion and Nova replace the Vanguard robots; characters sculpted as signed-distance fields

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20). Supersedes the geometry part of ADR-0069; its embedding, rig, clip, socket and licensing decisions stand.
- Context: the owner asked for the boxy Vanguard V-01/V-02 robots to be replaced with good-looking mascot characters, the way Unreal Engine ships Manny and Quinn: two realistic-proportion mannequins on one shared skeleton, with the third-person template defaulting to one of them (https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/third-person-template-in-unreal-engine). The robots were assembled from bevelled boxes in bpy; that approach cannot produce organic anatomy (deltoids, pectorals, knees, a face).
- Options: (a) hand-sculpt in Blender and commit only the binary; (b) download a licensed base mesh (MakeHuman CC0 output, Mixamo); (c) keep the generator-as-code approach but sculpt with signed-distance fields: smooth unions of round cones, ellipsoids and boxes, polygonised with marching cubes, decimated per piece to a budget.
- Decision: (c). `tools/characters/{sdf,anatomy,mesher,build_characters}.py` sculpt Orion (masculine, cyan) and Nova (feminine, amber) as android mannequins: a stylised face built on facial thirds, sculpted hair caps, white satin shell plates cut from the body field over a graphite undersuit, a glowing VICTUS chevron. The field is evaluated mirror-symmetric (smooth union is not associative, so evaluation order would otherwise break symmetry). Normals come from the field gradient, skin weights from primitive proximity limited to the dominant bone and its neighbours (smooth, at most four influences), materials are single-sided. The rig, 7 clips, 5 sockets, controller and ragdoll mapping are unchanged, so scripts, templates and the 16-body ragdoll keep working; only ids (`orion`, `nova`), paths, UUIDs, the controller filename (`victus_locomotion.vanim`) and the armature name (`Rig`) change. (a) is not reviewable and not regenerable; (b) brings third-party licensing and a non-VICTUS identity.
- Licensing: NumPy (BSD-3-Clause) and scikit-image (BSD-3-Clause) join Blender as build-time-only tools; nothing from them ships in the engine.
- Evidence: `tools/characters/test_sdf.py` (field symmetry, primitive distances, outward winding); `victus_asset_tests` starter-character cases (zero importer warnings, 4 meshes, 20k-35k triangles, 1.70 m, sockets parented to `Rig`, shared skeleton and bind pose, distinct designs); `victus_editor_tests`, `victus_project_tests`, `victus_render_tests` character LOD cases and the CLI smoke test.
- Consequences: no texture maps yet (shading detail is geometric); the face is a stylised mannequin face, not a scanned human; regenerating needs the bpy venv plus scikit-image. Projects created before this change keep their Vanguard files; new projects get Orion and Nova.

### ADR-0124: Receiver-aware shadow cascade culling

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20).
- Context: each cascade drew every caster inside its light-space box. That box is a sphere-fitted, texel-snapped square around the camera slice and extends toward the sun, so a caster beside the camera slice, or one already drawn by a nearer cascade whose shadow falls only where the nearer cascade wins, still cost its triangles in every cascade it touched. Unity culls casters per cascade with bounding spheres and its `ComputeVisibleSplitMask` checks enclosure in a previous cascade, reported as about 30 % fewer casters (https://discussions.unity.com/t/shadow-performance-and-quality-will-it-be-improved-will-we-ever-get-better-soft-shadows/845823?page=4); developers keep asking to skip casters already covered by nearer cascades (https://discussions.unity.com/t/culling-update-now-and-next/1741459/1).
- Decision: `RenderSettings::shadow_receiver_culling` (default on). `compute_cascades` records, per cascade, the region of the camera view whose pixels actually sample it: view depths from the start of the previous cascade's dithered transition band (`kCascadeTransition`, 10 % of the previous slice; cascade 0 starts at the near plane) to the split. That region is kept twice: as a light-space AABB and as a camera-slice frustum. Both are widened by `texel × (normal bias + 3)` to cover the shading normal offset and the PCF footprint. A caster is skipped for a cascade when its light-space box misses the receiver box in x/y or lies wholly below it, or when its shadow volume (the world box swept along the sun direction by its height above the lowest receiver) lies outside any plane of the slice frustum. The test is conservative: whatever a visible pixel of that cascade samples is still drawn. Skips are counted in `SceneStats::shadow_receiver_culled`.
- Evidence: "gpu shadows: receiver culling skips casters whose shadow no cascade pixel can see, image unchanged" in `victus_gpu_tests` (48 spheres, an off-screen pillar, 4 tiers × 3 sun directions): culling on and off produce pixel-identical images and the same main-view triangles, and every multi-cascade tier draws fewer shadow triangles (13-37 %). `victus_perf_budgets` (Medium, 640x360, frame 120): template-3d 34 537 → 23 180 shadow triangles (−33 %), template-multiplayer 20 316 → 15 459 (−24 %); main-view triangles unchanged. The scene instance buffer now grows with 1/8 headroom, because per-cascade culling makes the instance count vary by a few between frames and exact-fit growth doubled it (template-simulation 269 224 → 398 360 buffer bytes before the headroom, 285 240 after).
- Consequences: no image change and a CPU cost of six plane tests per caster per cascade. Casters inside a cascade transition band still draw in both cascades; per-cascade LOD selection from texel density is the next step. Point/spot shadow tiles are unaffected.

### ADR-0129: Stochastic rounding into the packed HDR targets

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20). Amends ADR-0126 (packed R11G11B10F HDR on phones and Low/Medium).
- Context: R11G11B10F keeps 6/6/5 mantissa bits, and the Vulkan specification leaves the rounding of conversions to smaller float formats undefined ("rounded to one or the other. The rounding mode is not defined", https://docs.vulkan.org/spec/latest/chapters/fundamentals.html). A truncating store loses half an ulp on average, biasing every channel dark (blue most, at 5 bits), and TAA re-stores its history every frame, so the loss compounds. Measured on lavapipe with plain stores: mean per-channel bias down to -0.55/255 in blue without TAA and -1.8/255 with 24 TAA frames, against RGBA16F. ADR-0126's test averages absolute differences over Low/Medium/High after two frames, which does not show a drift of this kind.
- Decision: when the scene colour is R11G11B10F, the opaque forward output (`pbr.frag`, so surface shaders too), the sky and both TAA resolves (`taa.frag`, `taa_upscale.frag`) round each channel stochastically to an exactly representable value, `floor(c / ulp + n) * ulp`, with the ulp from `frexp` (exponent clamped at -14, values clamped at 64 512) and `n` interleaved gradient noise (Jimenez 2014, https://www.iryoku.com/next-generation-post-processing-in-call-of-duty-advanced-warfare/) offset per channel and per frame. The stored value is then exact, so the result is unbiased under any hardware rounding mode, and the TAA history cannot drift. The flag (frame + 1, 0 = off) travels in spare uniform slots (`Lighting.screen.w`, `Sky.sun_dir.w`) and a new `quant` vector in both TAA uniform blocks.
- Evidence: `victus_gpu_tests` "gpu compact hdr: R11G11B10 scene colour matches RGBA16F at every tier, with less memory" (saturated, dark and emissive materials, a coloured point light, 24 TAA frames on High and Ultra): mean |difference| 0.15 / 0.26 / 0.42 / 0.41 8-bit steps (Low / Medium / High / Ultra), per-channel mean bias at most 0.38/255 (asserted below 0.5), under 0.1 % of channel samples off by more than 4/255 without TAA and under 2 % with TAA (the one-pixel horizon edge, where TAA converges differently), and fewer texture bytes. RGBA16F output is untouched (the flag is 0), so the Ultra reference goldens are unchanged.
- Consequences: a few ALU per pixel in four shaders, only on packed targets. Blended passes (particles, decals, water, fog and cloud composites, the bloom upsample) and the bloom downsample still store through the hardware path and are not dithered; their bias is inside the numbers above. The noise is below one 8-bit step after tonemapping; it was not judged on a phone panel.
- Amendment (2026-11-20): the macOS CI (Metal) failed the compact HDR test with a dark bias (mean |difference| 0.78 to 2.5 steps, blue bias about twice red and green, as for truncating stores). Metal compiles shaders with fast math by default, where `exp2` and division are approximate, so `floor(c / ulp + n) * ulp` was no longer exactly representable. The rounding now scales with `ldexp` in both directions, which is exact under any math mode; on lavapipe the results are bit-identical.

### ADR-0132: Per-cascade shadow LOD from cascade texel size

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20). Extends ADR-0077 (LOD hysteresis) and ADR-0124 (receiver-aware cascade culling).
- Context: every shadow pass drew the level picked for the main camera (plus `shadow_lod_bias`). A caster near the camera is at LOD0 in the main view, but its shadow can also land in the wide cascades, where one texel spans several centimetres, so those cascades rasterised full-detail silhouettes they cannot resolve. After ADR-0124 the 3D template still drew 23.2k shadow triangles against 11.6k in the main view. Unreal exposes the same trade as a forced shadow LOD for cascaded shadow maps (`r.ForceLODShadow`) and a Nanite shadow LOD bias for virtual shadow maps (https://tomlooman.com/unreal-engine-optimization-talk/), and its virtual shadow maps choose shadow resolution per view from projected texel size (https://docs.unrealengine.com/en-US/Engine/Rendering/LightingAndShadows/Shadows/index.html).
- Decision: `RenderSettings::shadow_lod_texels` (default 1, 0 disables, sanitised to 0..8). For every caster accepted by directional cascade c, `cascade_shadow_lod` walks from the draw's `shadow_lod` towards coarser levels while the next level's geometric error (meshoptimizer's error in mesh units, times the model's largest axis scale) is at most `shadow_lod_texels` of that cascade's texel size (`cascade_texel_[c]`, the snapped orthographic texel width). The result goes to `Draw::cascade_lod[c]`; cascade batches sort and instance on it. The main-view level plus the bias stays the floor, so a cascade never draws finer than before; local light tiles and the rain occluder keep `shadow_lod`; `force_lod` still forces every pass. One texel of error stays inside the 1-texel receiver normal offset (`kShadowNormalBiasTexels`) and the 3x3 PCF footprint, so the stored depth moves by less than the filter already blurs.
- Evidence: `victus_gpu_tests` "gpu shadows: per-cascade LOD cuts shadow triangles without changing the image" (40 detailed spheres, low sun, 384x216, Low to Ultra): main-view triangles identical; shadow triangles 56 400 to 51 892 (Medium), 67 680 to 60 918 (High), 78 960 to 72 198 (Ultra), unchanged on Low's single cascade; mean image difference at most 0.005 8-bit levels, at most 20 of 331 776 channels change by more than 8, all on shadow edges. `victus_perf_budgets` (Medium, 640x360): template-3d 23 180 to 19 094 shadow triangles (-18 %), template-multiplayer 15 459 to 14 457, sample-arena 28 328 to 24 178 (-15 %), main-view triangles unchanged.
- Consequences: a short loop over at most three levels per caster per cascade on the CPU. The pick is stateless per cascade: as the camera moves, a caster can switch level inside a wide cascade, and that change is under one texel. Error is measured on the bind pose, so a skinned caster's pose is not considered. Local (point and spot) shadow tiles do not use it yet.

### ADR-0145: Lightmap bounce colour from the albedo texture mean

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20). Amends ADR-0122 (baked lighting).
- Context: the bake path tracer used only each material's constant base colour factor. Imported glTF models (photoscanned props, CC0 interior kits) almost always carry a white factor and put the colour in the base colour texture, so every textured wall, floor and prop bounced light as if it were white: baked interiors came out too bright and colourless, and coloured surfaces did not bleed into their neighbours.
- Decision: `texture_mean_color(const CookedTexture&)` averages the smallest stored mip (decoded from sRGB when the texture is sRGB), returning white for an empty or damaged texture. The bake multiplies each glTF model material's base colour factor by that mean. Per-texel albedo lookup was not chosen: the bounce colour is low frequency, and the mean costs one small read per material instead of a UV lookup at every path vertex.
- Evidence: `victus_gpu_tests` "scene lightmap: albedo texture mean for bounce colour (ADR-0145)" (a red-textured floor with a white factor tints the bounce on a white wall red; an empty texture gives white): with the existing lightmap case, 2 cases / 10 assertions pass. The game Project- baked Op04, Op05 and Op07 with it.
- Consequences: `.vmat` material textures are still not sampled (constant factor only); texture detail does not modulate bounce within a surface.

### ADR-0146: Camera-lens imperfections in the tonemap pass

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20).
- Context: games that sell "real footage" (body-cam and found-footage shooters such as Bodycam and Unrecord) rely on the camera's own defects as much as on lighting: wide-lens barrel, lateral colour fringing and sensor noise in the dark. The renderer had only a vignette. The game Project- (a chest-camera night shooter) asked for that look on phones, so the cost must be a few ALU and at most two extra texture reads in a pass that already runs.
- Decision: three `Environment` / `EnvironmentComponent` fields, all 0 by default: `lens_distortion` (-1..1; Brown-Conrady k1 barrel, `uv = 0.5 + d (1 + k r^2) / (1 + k/2)` with k = 0.25 x value, so the frame is zoomed to keep the corners on screen; negative is pincushion), `chromatic_aberration` (0..1; red and blue read at +/- 0.6 % of the offset from centre, so fringes grow toward the edges and the centre is untouched) and `film_grain` (0..1; triangular zero-mean noise from an integer hash of pixel and a per-render frame counter, added in square-root space with amplitude 0.05 x value, reduced to 35 % in the highlights). All three run in `tonemap.frag` (no new pass, no render target); bloom samples through the same distorted UV. Probe captures (`capture_mode`) force them off. Grain is applied after grading and vignette, before sRGB encoding and FXAA.
- Alternatives: a separate post pass (an extra full-screen read/write on tile GPUs, rejected); blue-noise texture grain (a texture binding and memory for a look the hash already gives); grain in linear light (tried first: at 0.42 it buried the night scenes in noise, because a linear step near black is tens of 8-bit levels).
- Evidence: `victus_gpu_tests` "gpu quality: lens distortion, chromatic aberration and film grain (ADR-0146)" (lavapipe, gcc 15.2.0): no colour fringes with everything off; barrel magnifies a centred cube (> 5 % more lit pixels), pincushion shrinks it; CA produces > 40 fringe pixels and leaves the centre pixel identical; grain gives > 4 distinct values on a flat face, mean shift < 3 levels, and changes between frames: 22/22 assertions. "gles: every built-in shader with GLSL ES compiles on the device" and the `gpu quality*`, golden and reference cases still pass (29 cases, 814 assertions).
- Consequences: two extra `u_hdr` reads only with CA on; distortion slightly softens the image (bilinear resample) and is not applied to UI. Grain is per render, so at a fixed frame rate it animates at that rate. FXAA/TAA see the grain only on the FXAA path (FXAA runs after tonemap); TAA runs before tonemap and is unaffected.


### ADR-0147: Depth-based disocclusion in the TAA and temporal upscaling resolves

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20). Extends ADR-0055 (temporal upscaling), ADR-0142 and ADR-0143 (reactive mask); ROADMAP "ASR/FSR2 ideas" item.
- Context: both resolves reject stale history only through the YCoCg neighbourhood clip. Where a surface is uncovered (camera or an occluder moved) and the occluder's colour lies inside the uncovered background's colour range (a checker, foliage, a brick wall), the clip keeps the occluder's history and it ghosts for many frames. FSR2 solves this with a depth clip: it reconstructs the previous frame's depth by scattering this frame's depth through the motion vectors with atomics, then compares it to the current depth with a separation tolerance scaled by view width, field of view and depth ([FSR2 manual](https://gpuopen.com/manuals/fidelityfx_sdk/techniques/super-resolution-temporal/); tolerance form quoted from `ffx_fsr2_depth_clip.h` in [this survey](https://github.com/DennisSmolek/ssgiDev/blob/main/research/sources/temporal/02-disocclusion-history-rejection.md)). The scatter needs compute and atomics, which the GLES backend (ADR-0144) does not have.
- Decision: a gather form that runs in the existing fragment resolves on every backend. The resolve writes a second colour target, the linear view depth of the pixel's own (undilated) depth, into a ping-ponged R16F (else R32F) depth history at history resolution; the linear-depth coefficients are set every frame, also before a history exists, so frame one compares against a valid depth. Next frame it takes the current closest depth through the camera reprojection into the previous view (the depth that surface had then, for static geometry; the far plane maps to itself, because through the inverted matrices `1 / (a d + b)` with `a d + b` near zero loses its precision and flagged the sky), and reads the farthest of the 3x3 depth-history texels around the reprojected uv. If that farthest texel is nearer than the reprojected depth by more than the tolerance, the surface was hidden last frame and the factor is `1 - (tolerance / separation)^2`; the current-frame weight becomes `mix(alpha, 1, factor * taa_disocclusion)` (a full reset; a smaller clip box or a partial weight measured worse). Tolerance per unit depth: FSR2's `Ksep * Kfov * half_width` (Ksep 1.37e-5, Kfov = |corner| / |centre| of the view, half the history width in pixels), floored at 0.5 % for R16F storage and depth-buffer precision. The farthest of a 3x3 footprint and the undilated stored depth came from measurement: a dilated history or a 2x2 bilinear vote flagged sphere silhouettes, contact points and thin posts in the static reference scenes, whose depth edge moves up to a pixel with the jitter. Pixels with object motion are left to the colour clip, because the camera-only reprojected depth is wrong for surfaces that moved along the view axis (a receding object would read as disoccluded). `RenderSettings::taa_disocclusion` (0..1, default 1) turns it down or off; `SceneRenderStats::taa_disocclusion` reports it; without a float colour target (R16F or R32F) it is off.
- Alternatives: FSR2's scatter (compute + atomics, not on GLES, two more passes); comparing the current depth with last frame's depth at the same uv without reprojecting it, which [pmndrs/upscaler measured](https://github.com/pmndrs/upscaler/blob/main/CLAUDE.md) as disoccluding most of a wall during a dolly; colour-only rejection (status quo).
- Evidence (lavapipe, `gpu: taa depth disocclusion stops occluder-coloured ghosts`): a red occluder 3 units away in front of 2-pixel red and blue stripes 8 units away, camera jumps 1.5 units sideways; mean RGB error against a fresh converged reference, inside the analytically disoccluded wall (624 pixels at 96x96), summed over the four frames after the move: native 61.6 with the test vs 169.0 without (0.36x), temporal upscaling at render scale 0.5 84.9 vs 160.6 (0.53x). A static camera is unchanged (difference 0). The four reference scenes match their goldens without regenerating them (exterior, effects and photoreal FLIP 0, interior 1.3e-6): the static views flag nothing, and the rotating objects carry object motion. GLES-forced GPU suite: 199/199 cases.

### ADR-0148: Thin-feature locks in the TAA and temporal upscaling resolves

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20). Extends ADR-0147 (depth-history target), ADR-0055, ADR-0142, ADR-0143; ROADMAP "ASR/FSR2 ideas" item.
- Context: wires, cables, fences and distant railings narrower than a pixel are hit by only some of the jitter phases. In the others the current 3x3 neighbourhood holds only the background, the YCoCg clip moves the history to it, and the feature breaks into flickering dashes. Measured before this change (new `gpu: taa keeps sub-pixel wires` case, dark wires 0.17 to 0.5 output pixels wide on white, against an 8x8 supersampled ground truth): the wires keep 11.5 of their true 68.2 darkness at native resolution and 3.9 when upscaling from half resolution. FSR2 handles this with locks: a pixel whose luminance is a ridge in its 3x3 neighbourhood (brighter or darker than every dissimilar neighbour, and no 2x2 quadrant through it uniformly similar, so edges are excluded) gets a lock with a lifetime of one jitter period; a locked pixel blends its history towards the unclipped value ([FSR2 manual](https://gpuopen.com/manuals/fidelityfx_sdk/techniques/super-resolution-temporal/), `ComputeThinFeatureConfidence` in `ffx_fsr2_lock.h`). Unreal 5.6 and 5.7 added TSR thin-geometry detection for the same artefact: detected lines relax the history clamp ([Epic](https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/thin-geometry-detection-with-temporal-super-resolution)).
- Decision: locks in the existing fragment resolves, stored in the green channel of the ADR-0147 depth history, which becomes RG16F (else RG32F). The ridge test is FSR2's (similarity ratio 1.05, the four quadrant masks) on the luminance-weighted luma of the current 3x3 render-texel neighbourhood that the resolve already reads. A ridge sets the lifetime to 1; otherwise the lock reprojected from last frame (nearest texel at the reprojected uv) loses 1/8 per frame. FSR2 uses one jitter period, but the upscaler's 32-frame period at half resolution held a vanished wire for 10+ frames (ghost darkness 23 of 255). Disocclusion (ADR-0147), the reactive mask (ADR-0143) and object motion clear it; with object motion the unclipped history trailed the moving comb in the motion-vector test. The clipped history moves back towards the unclipped one by `saturate(4 * lifetime) * taa_thin_features * (1 - disocclusion)`. `RenderSettings::taa_thin_features` (0..1, default 1) turns it down or off; `SceneRenderStats::taa_thin_features` reports it. Fragment-only, so it also runs on GLES.
- Alternatives: FSR2's separate lock pass at render resolution with a scattered new-lock mask (an extra pass and an R8 target, the same test); Unreal's coverage map and spatial diffusion (several compute passes, needs shading-model IDs); a wider clip box everywhere (ghosts on all surfaces).
- Evidence (lavapipe, measured in linear light; the post pass encodes sRGB into the UNORM target, so test images are linearised before comparison): `gpu: taa keeps sub-pixel wires (ADR-0148)`, mean error against the 8x8 supersampled truth over 16 converged frames in the 1 234 wire pixels: native 47.0 to 36.0 (0.76x), flicker 22.4 to 9.6, wire darkness 23.5 to 98.0 of the true 68.2 (the luminance-weighted blend over-darkens a dark wire somewhat; without locks it nearly vanishes); half-resolution upscaling 60.4 to 25.1 (0.42x), flicker 11.6 to 7.2, darkness 8.6 to 67.8. `gpu thin geometry: sub-pixel poles keep their coverage` (white pole on black): TAA energy relative to point sampling went from 0.16x to 0.72x at 0.5 px and from 0.10x to 0.62x at 0.25 px, with row continuity 1.0 and 0.93 (point sampling 0.52 and 0.27). That test previously asserted TAA energy below point sampling, which encoded the defect. Ten frames after the wires vanish nothing remains (ghost 0). Reference scenes against a 4x4 supersampled render of the same scene (1280x720, box-filtered to 320x180 in encoded space), frames 15 and 30, before and after: exterior FLIP 0.0215/0.0214 to 0.0205/0.0201, worst-tile SSIM 0.964 to 0.988, PSNR +1.3/+1.7 dB; photoreal FLIP 0.0596/0.0595 to 0.0575/0.0573, SSIM 0.918 to 0.938; effects PSNR +0.3/+0.2 dB; interior neutral (-0.15/-0.07 dB). The lavapipe goldens were regenerated for this change.

### ADR-0149: Per-platform texture profiles in game exports

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20). Extends ADR-0047 (block-compressed variants) and the Android shell (docs/ANDROID.md).
- Context: the import cache keeps every texture as a full RGBA8 mip chain plus BC and ASTC variants, so one cache serves the editor on any GPU. `victus export` shipped that cache verbatim. For a project with many 2048 px photoscan textures (the Special Ops showcase: nine characters with six 2048 px maps each) the pack was 2.29 GB against 214 MB of source assets. The Android shell copies the pack to internal storage once, so a phone needed about 4.6 GB, and a single APK asset entry above 2 GB is fragile in tooling. A phone at 720p cannot resolve 2048 px detail on a character, and the RGBA8 copy of a level that the GPU samples as ASTC is never uploaded.
- Decision: `victus export --target all|desktop|android` (`ExportOptions::target`, `assets::texture_export_profile`). `all` (default) ships the cache unchanged. `desktop` drops the ASTC variants. `android` drops the BC variants, drops top mips above 1024 px, and stores levels above 64 px only as ASTC (cooked format v9: each RGBA8 level carries a stored flag; a level without pixels needs a compressed variant, otherwise the file is rejected as corrupt). Small levels keep RGBA8 for CPU users (the lightmap baker averages the smallest mip). HDR textures are untouched. The runtime uploads the first variant the device samples. If none qualifies (no ASTC sampling, or compression disabled), `upload_cooked_texture` decodes the levels on the CPU with astcenc (`assets::rgba8_level`), so a stripped texture always renders. Textures imported without an ASTC variant keep all their RGBA8 levels.
- Alternatives: lowering `max_texture_size` in every meta (the desktop build loses detail); dropping only BC (the RGBA8 copy is 4x the ASTC size and stays the bulk); ETC2 (SDL_GPU exposes no ETC2 formats, ADR-0047); recompressing to ASTC 6x6 at export (a second lossy pass; can be added as a profile option later).
- Evidence: `victus_asset_tests` "texture export profiles: android keeps ASTC, caps size and strips large RGBA8 levels" (size cap, BC dropped, the stripped file under a quarter of the original, CPU decode of a stripped level at 32 dB or better PSNR against its RGBA8 source, kept levels verbatim, a stripped level with no variant rejected, HDR and ASTC-less textures untouched); `victus_export_tests` "export: the android target ships ASTC-only textures that still load and decode" (packaged-mode load, manifest `target`). The CPU-decode upload path has no GPU test yet.
- Consequences: cooked format v9 re-imports existing caches once. An Android pack is not meant for desktop GPUs without ASTC: they take the CPU decode at load. The 1024 px cap applies to every texture, including UI art; per-asset overrides are future work.

### ADR-0150: Android orientation follows the manifest; tier foliage range; allocation-free LOD history

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20). Extends ADR-0077 (LOD hysteresis), the Android shell (docs/ANDROID.md) and the quality tiers.
- Context: (1) The first device report of the Special Ops APK (Infinix Hot 10S) showed the game in portrait although the manifest asks for `sensorLandscape`. SDL replaces the activity orientation when it creates the window (`SDLActivity.setOrientationBis`): for a resizable window without `SDL_HINT_ORIENTATIONS` it requests `SCREEN_ORIENTATION_FULL_USER`, which follows the phone's rotation lock. (2) Profiling the showcase forest at Low (the phone tier) with new per-stage zones in `SceneRenderer::render` (`render.cpu.*`) put 2.41 ms of the 4.69 ms renderer CPU time in visibility, for 7 266 submitted draws. Most of it was the LOD hysteresis history, a `std::unordered_map` that was cleared and refilled every frame, one node allocation per static draw, and a second stateless `select_lod` per held draw. (3) Low and Medium drew scattered foliage to the full authored range and let every instance cast shadows, although a 1024 px single cascade cannot resolve far grass.
- Decision: `VictusActivity` reads its manifest `screenOrientation` and re-applies it in `onCreate` and in its `setOrientationBis` override; `unspecified` falls back to SDL's rule. The LOD history is an open-addressing table of 64-bit keys (power-of-two capacity, at least twice the draw count, cleared with one fill), with the same first-insert-wins semantics, and `select_lod` returns the stateless pick through an out parameter. New `RenderSettings::foliage_distance_scale` (0.25..1) and `foliage_shadow_fraction` (0..1): foliage draws within `draw_distance * scale`, and only instances within `fraction` of that range cast shadows; the rest are submitted with a shadowless copy of the material (the cast-shadow flag is part of the batch key, so they batch separately). Low 0.7 / 0.5, Medium 0.85 / 0.75, High and Ultra 1 / 1 (unchanged). The profile report gains `frame.submitted`, `frame.culled`, `frame.shadow_casters` and `frame.reflection_probes`.
- Alternatives: setting `SDL_HINT_ORIENTATIONS` from C++ (duplicates the Gradle property in native code); a sorted vector for the history (an n log n sort per frame); thinning foliage by density instead of range (same instance count, more visible gaps near the player).
- Evidence (lavapipe, gcc 15.2.0, Special Ops `op01_forest`, Low, 410x180, 300 frames, means): `render.cpu.visibility` 2.41 to 0.97 ms, `SceneRenderer::render` 4.69 to 3.48 ms (p95 5.33 to 3.55 ms), with the same 7 266 submitted draws and 1 138 829 triangles. The foliage range alone lowered alpha-tested draws 1 482 to 1 069 and triangles 1.20 M to 1.14 M in that view. `victus_gpu_tests` "gpu: foliage draws its mesh renderer ..." extended: half range with zero shadow fraction keeps 263 of 1 607 instances and 4 of 1 110 shadow casters (terrain only), shadow triangles 17 900 to 4 628; sanitising clamps NaN and out-of-range values. Full suite 200/200 cases. The orientation change compiles into the release APK; it has not been seen on a device yet.
- Consequences: Low and Medium show less far grass and no shadows from far grass. Projects that need the old range can set both fields to 1.

### ADR-0151: Card LODs for leaf canopies; Low-tier foliage LOD bias

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-20). Extends ADR-0049 (mesh LODs), ADR-0077 (LOD hysteresis), ADR-0132 (shadow LODs) and the quality tiers.
- Context: after ADR-0150 the Special Ops forest at Low still drew 1 138 829 main-view and 514 162 shadow triangles per frame, too much for a phone GPU such as the Mali-G52 MC2 (Infinix Hot 10S). Per-draw attribution showed about 850 k of the main-view triangles in alpha-tested tree canopies (1 224 to 1 476 triangles each, 600+ trees in view) drawn at LOD 0 up to 150 m away. Each canopy is 454 to 738 disconnected two-triangle cards with radii of about 0.9 to 1.8 m. meshoptimizer's edge-collapse simplifier cannot reduce such a mesh: every card is a border, so it built no level for most canopies and one weak level for the others. Raising `lod_error_pixels` from 2 to 4 changed the triangle count by under 2 %. A spatial-fidelity experiment in the same session (geometric specular AA from normal derivatives, Tokuyoshi/Kaplanyan style) was rejected: on a bumpy polished-metal test floor its mean error against a 4x4 supersampled reference was 11.6 to 52 against 5.0 without it, at every strength tried, because it spreads clipped highlights.
- Decision: `generate_lods` first tries card LODs: a non-skinned mesh without morph targets made of at least 32 disconnected pieces, at most 4 triangles each on average. Level n keeps the first `floor(pieces * ratio^n)` pieces of a fixed splitmix64 order (nested subsets, so coarser levels only remove cards) and grows each kept piece about its centroid by `1/sqrt(kept fraction)`, which keeps the canopy's card area. The grown cards are appended vertices; LOD 0 and `indices` (collision, navigation) are unchanged. The level error is the largest distance a grown card's corner moved. That metric does not capture the removed cards, so new `RenderSettings::foliage_lod_bias` (0..3) lets a tier take coarser levels for alpha-tested draws on purpose, applied after the hysteresis pick (the history keeps the unbiased level); shadows add `shadow_lod_bias` on top. Low 1, other tiers 0. Model importer version 7 re-imports models once.
- Alternatives: impostors or billboard clouds for far trees (a baking pipeline and new atlas assets; future work); culling trees by distance on Low (holes in the treeline through the fog); a larger `lod_error_pixels` (no effect on card meshes); geometric specular AA (measured worse, above).
- Evidence (lavapipe, `op01_forest`, Low, 410x180, 60 frames): main-view triangles 1 138 829 to 608 499 (-47 %), shadow triangles 514 162 to 156 728 (-70 %), 1.65 M to 0.77 M in total; draw calls 442 to 455 (more LOD levels in the instancing keys). At Low 1640x720 the opening view with and without the bias differs only in the density of the nearest canopy. `victus_render_tests` new cases "mesh lods: card canopies thin to nested subsets of grown cards" (level sizes, area within 2 %, error formula, nesting, optimize_mesh keeps the triangles) and "connected meshes and few cards get no card levels"; full suite 66/66; `victus_gpu_tests` 200/200, `victus_asset_tests` 38/38, `victus_robustness_tests` 15/15.
- Consequences: card meshes grow by up to 0.875x their vertex count and their bounds by the grown cards. Canopies on Low look slightly thinner and coarser close up. Phone frame times are not measured here.

### ADR-0152: 4x MSAA with alpha to coverage on the Low tier

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-21). Extends ADR-0035 (quality tiers), the forward renderer and the render graph; complements ADR-0150/0151 (Low-tier image stability).
- Context: a phone capture of the Special Ops prologue at Low (Infinix Hot 10S, 1640x720, `render_scale` 0.75, FXAA, no TAA) showed crawling edges and glittering grass and leaf cards when the player turned. Low has no TAA because history at 0.75 scale smears on phones; FXAA alone cannot rebuild the coverage of thin alpha-tested cards, which flicker on and off between frames. Tile-based phone GPUs (Mali, Adreno) resolve multisampling inside tile memory, so MSAA is the usual mobile answer where TAA is too costly or too soft.
- Decision: new `RenderSettings::msaa_samples` (1/2/4/8, sanitized to the nearest power of two; Low 4, other tiers 1) and `alpha_to_coverage` (default on). When TAA is off, no debug view is active and the device reports the sample count for the HDR and depth formats (`rhi::Device::supports_samples`, cached per format), the opaque forward pass renders into transient multisampled HDR and depth targets and resolves colour into the single-sample HDR target (`RenderGraph::write_color_resolved`; multisampled depth is not kept). Forward pipelines are remembered at creation and get multisampled twins on first use. Alpha-tested materials draw with `PipelineDesc::alpha_to_coverage` (SDL_GPU `enable_alpha_to_coverage`, GLES `GL_SAMPLE_ALPHA_TO_COVERAGE`) and `pbr.frag` replaces the hard cutoff with a one-pixel coverage ramp `clamp((a - cutoff) / fwidth(a) + 0.5)`. Passes that sample depth (SSAO, contact shadows, fog, decals, water) need a single-sample depth, so MSAA forces the depth pre-pass in that case; on Low nothing samples depth and Low keeps no pre-pass. TAA tiers keep `msaa_samples = 1`. The render-path report lists `msaa` (active `4x, alpha to coverage`, or why it is off). `victus run --msaa off|2|4|8|auto`.
- Alternatives: TAA on Low (ghosting and softness at 0.75 scale, history memory); SMAA (a post filter, it cannot restore sub-pixel cards); supersampling (4x the shading cost); alpha-to-coverage without MSAA (no samples to cover, measured no gain); sharpening the cutoff or blurring the alpha mips (changes the look of every card, does not stop popping).
- Evidence (lavapipe, `victus_gpu_tests` new file `tests/render/gpu_msaa_tests.cpp`, error against a 4x4 supersampled reference box-filtered in linear light, tonemap off): a rotated opaque box edge 0.697 at 1x to 0.342 with 4x MSAA; a field of thin alpha-tested cards 16.43 at 1x, 16.44 with MSAA alone and 10.80 with MSAA plus alpha to coverage; TAA tiers report 1 sample; presets and sanitizing as above. `render_graph_tests` resolve case; `gpu_quality_tests` checks Low has `msaa_samples == 4` and no depth pre-pass. `victus_render_tests` 67/67, `victus_gpu_tests` 202/202, `victus_robustness_tests` 15/15.
- Consequences: Low costs one 4x HDR (R11G11B10F) and one 4x depth transient (about 4x the single-sample size on desktop GPUs; on tile-based GPUs largely on-chip). Every forward pipeline may exist twice. Alpha-tested edges are softer. The phone frame-time cost is not measured here.

### ADR-0153: Lambert and environment-BRDF terms stay FP32 in the FP16 PBR variant

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-21). Amends ADR-0137.
- Context: CI's Linux Release lane failed `victus_reference_scenes` on every push from d1f895b to 2096152: the photoreal scene rendered with `--fp16 on` scored mean FLIP 0.0050 to 0.0052 against the FP32 golden, above the 0.005 limit, while the local lavapipe (newer Mesa, GCC 15) scored 0.0038. The FP32 renders matched the golden exactly on both, so the drift is the half-precision arithmetic, rounded differently by two llvmpipe versions. Bisecting the FP16 terms on photoreal with TAA off (FP16 vs FP32, mean FLIP): all FP16 0.0045; computing the Smith visibility directly instead of dividing by 4 NdotV NdotL 0.0045 (no gain, not adopted); minimum roughness 0.045 instead of 0.089 0.0045 (no gain); `env_brdf` in FP32 0.0035; Lambert term in FP32 0.0030; whole `brdf()` in FP32 0.0026. The errors were 1 to 2 LSB shifts over large sunlit areas (SSIM 0.9997, PSNR 56 dB), not visible, but they are what FLIP's mean adds up.
- Decision: `env_brdf` (Karis' analytic split-sum fit, five ALU ops) is FP32 in every variant: its scale and bias multiply the whole environment specular, and its bias `1.04 * a004 + r.w` cancels most of its half-precision bits. In `brdf()` the diffuse weight `(1 - F)(1 - metallic)` is still computed in half, but the Lambert product with albedo and NdotL is FP32 (three multiplies per light). The GGX D, Smith G and Schlick F terms, which carry the registers and transcendental-free ALU that FP16 saves, stay half precision with the 0.089 clamp. The FP32 shader is unchanged (its code is identical; the goldens still match with FLIP 0).
- Alternatives: raising the FP16 tolerance (hides a measurable error and leaves no margin for the next driver); FP16 off in the reference test (loses the only gate on the variant); keeping FP16 everywhere and recording per-driver goldens (needs a golden per Mesa version).
- Evidence (lavapipe, Mesa from Ubuntu 26.04, Release, 320x180 ultra, frames 15 and 30, FP16 vs FP32 mean FLIP, TAA on / off): photoreal 0.0038 / 0.0045 to 0.0017 / 0.0017; exterior 0.0027 to 0.0014 / 0.0008; interior 0.0034 to 0.0021 / 0.0007; effects 0.0039 to 0.0014 / 0.0010. The CI Mesa is expected to drop by a similar factor (from 0.0052); this is confirmed only once CI runs.
- Consequences: three FP32 multiplies per light and five per pixel in the FP16 variant, a negligible share of the BRDF; the speed-up of the variant on phone GPUs is still unmeasured (ADR-0137).

### ADR-0154: `.vpak` version 2 — per-entry zstd, BLAKE2b-256 digests, Ed25519 signatures

- Status: Accepted (2026-11-21). Extends the pack format of Slice 18; closes the compression and pack-signing parts of production blocker B6.
